# AI and Virtual Agent Laws, Court Cases and Compliance Standards

**Version:** 1.11 (November 25, 2026). Full update from parallel research on US federal, state, court, EU and UK, Asia-Pacific, Latin American, African and Middle Eastern, and standards and messaging sources (see the 1.11 change note). Version 1.10 (November 22, 2026). Conflict-resolution revision on top of 1.9: resolved or narrowed the Part 4 conflicts against primary and secondary sources (see the 1.10 change note). Version 1.9 (November 22, 2026). Review-driven revision: corrected the Colorado, Oregon, Minnesota, Vermont, Garcia, Harper, FCC revocation, AB 1609, NYC and Digital Fairness Act entries; fixed the status labels and verification tiers; added the Genesys Cloud on-ramp, Section 1.12, Sections 3.15 to 3.20, a corrected test-ID map (catalog version 1.1) and Appendix A. Version 1.8 (November 18, 2026) added Section 1.11, Section 2.17 and Section 3.14.
**Scope:** Laws and regulations (US local, state and federal; EU and EU member states; UK; and other countries worldwide), with maximum penalties that govern the use of AI or virtual agents; US and international court cases and enforcement decisions in which AI was part of the case; and PCI, HIPAA and other organizational compliance standards that address AI and virtual agents.
**Companion file:** *Virtual Agent Corner-Case Test Catalog* (Virtual_Agent_Corner_Case_Test_Catalog.md, version 1.1, in the same folder as this document). Categories M, P and Q turn many of these requirements into test cases. Keep the two files together, because test IDs in this document refer to that version.

> **Not legal advice, and not test evidence.** This is a research compendium built from law-firm, regulator, court and news sources. Items marked [T1] were checked against primary text on November 22, 2026. Other rows are current to November 18, 2026 unless they carry a later note. It is not exhaustive: US states alone introduced more than 1,500 AI bills in 2026. It is not a compliance certification, and passing the test catalog does not establish compliance. Status labels and dates change quickly, and several sources conflict. Escalate any Pending or (verify) item to counsel before acting on it. Content owner: [assign a named owner]. Review cadence: monthly, and before each release of a virtual agent.

**Status labels (fixed set; use only these)**

| Label | Meaning |
|---|---|
| **In force** | Currently applies |
| **Enacted, not yet effective** | Signed or adopted, with a future effective or application date |
| **Enjoined or stayed** | Enforcement blocked or paused by a court or by agreement |
| **Pending** | Bill, proposal or rule moving through a legislative or rulemaking process |
| **Guidance** | Regulator guidance, policy statement or voluntary code; not binding law, but used in enforcement |
| **Repealed or replaced** | Superseded by later law, which is named |
| **Vetoed or died** | Not law (vetoed, failed or expired) |

**Verification tiers**

| Tier | Meaning | Marker |
|---|---|---|
| T1, primary | Checked against the statute, Federal Register, official regulator or court page | [T1] |
| T2, secondary | Law-firm, news, tracker or vendor sources only | (verify) |
| T3, background | Background knowledge, not searched in the current round | (verify) |

Existing (verify) markers are read as T2 or T3 until a row is re-tiered. Trackers (RecordingLaw, LawSignals, CASRAI, ailawsuittracker and similar) support only T2 claims and must not be the sole basis for an In force label.

**What changed in version 1.11 (November 25, 2026).** Updated by seven parallel research passes. Largest changes: the EU Digital Omnibus on AI is Regulation (EU) 2026/1744, in force July 27, 2026, with the Article 50(2) marking grace for existing systems ending December 2, 2026; Commission Implementing Decision (EU) 2026/2274 cites EN 18286:2026, the first AI Act harmonised standard; the Digital Fairness Act text was read (Article 25 human-communication right, proposed as a Regulation repealing the UCPD); the Digital Omnibus on data Council text reinstates the GDPR Article 22 default prohibition; the UK DUAA Articles 22A to 22D are in force from February 5, 2026; the FCC revocation order (FCC 26-67) is in force November 25, 2026, with comments on the further notice due that day and replies due December 28; Colorado SB 26-189 is Chapter 131 and the Attorney General adopted its rules on November 19, 2026; the Colorado HB 26-1263 general date passed on August 12, 2026 unless a referendum was filed, and operator requirements start January 1, 2027; New York A 3411 was vetoed November 20, 2026; Washington HB 2225 and Oregon SB 1546 text was checked; Montana MCA 45-8-213 was read; the NYC DCWP page now lists December 18, 2026 for the hearing and comments, conflicting with the November 19 notice, so the window is treated as open pending confirmation; Oklahoma, Alabama and Louisiana enacted comprehensive privacy laws in 2026 (the privacy count is 23, and MultiState's 24 includes Florida's narrower law); Garcia's August 4 extension is still unconfirmed; Thomson Reuters v. Ross was affirmed by the Third Circuit on September 29, 2026; the Harper Rule 12(c) order is confirmed; Barrows, Kisting-Leung and Lokken schedules are confirmed; Bartz final approval is confirmed; Kadrey's appeal request has no ruling; Chile's Law 21.719 postponement passed the Chamber on November 17 but is not yet published, so December 1, 2026 still governs; Brazil's MP 1.396/2026 has a first term ending December 11, 2026; Canada's C-36 is at first reading and C-34 is in committee; India's DPDP consent-manager framework is in force from November 13, 2026, and the TRAI Third Amendment was notified September 18, 2026; Japan's APPI amendments await a cabinet order for commencement; Russia's Federal Law 243-FZ was signed July 26, 2026. Newly added: Garante's EUR 158,000 decision against Character.AI, the reported Dutch AP decision against Uber, the Irish DPC inquiry into Grok, several 2026 state AG and court items, the FTC AI-accuracy proposal, the FINRA Notice 26-14 scope, Ofcom's mobile messaging scam rules, the CMA and DRCF agentic-AI statement, and the Australia eSafety age-restricted codes. Several research passes ran out of search budget; the gaps are listed in Part 4 and are not yet verified.

## Top obligations for a Genesys Cloud virtual agent (as of November 22, 2026)

This table is a starting list, not a complete one. Each row points to the section that holds the detail and to the catalog test that checks it. Confirm each row with counsel for your markets.

| Obligation | Where it applies | Date | Owner | Test ID |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Tell users they are talking to an AI, at the start and on request | California B.O.T. Act; Utah; Maine; Colorado HB 26-1263; EU AI Act Article 50(1); NYC proposal | In force now (EU from August 2, 2026); Colorado HB 26-1263 date disputed (August 12, 2026 unless a referendum petition was filed; January 1, 2027 for some duties per other sources) | Design | M1 |
| Offer a path to a human within the stated time limits | California AB 1609; NYC proposal; Brazil Decree 13.135/2026 | January 1, 2027 (AB 1609); proposals pending | Design and operations | F3, F6 |
| Honor opt-outs and revocations on every channel | FCC consent-revocation order; TCPA; CAN-SPAM; PECR; CASL | November 25, 2026 (Federal Register text, T1) | Operations | M4, Q4 |
| Check consent, registries and calling windows before each campaign | TCPA; state mini-TCPA laws; Colombia Law 2300; Australia Do Not Call Register; UK TPS and CTPS | In force | Campaign operations | M3, Q5, Q6 |
| Disclose an artificial voice on automated calls | California AB 2905 | In force since January 1, 2025 | Design | M1 |
| Give notice before recording or AI analysis starts | CIPA; all-party consent states; GDPR Article 13 | In force | Design and legal | M2 |
| Explain and review decisions made from customer messages | GDPR Article 22; Australia APPs 1.7 to 1.9; Colorado SB 26-189 | Australia from December 10, 2026 | Product and compliance | M5 |
| Label synthetic content | EU AI Act Article 50(2); China labeling measures; South Korea AI Basic Act | EU grace period for systems already on the market ends December 2, 2026 | Content and engineering | Q2 |
| Keep sender identity and authentication correct for SMS and email | CAN-SPAM; CASL; Spam Act 2003; Gmail, Yahoo and Microsoft rules; India DLT | In force | Marketing operations | Q1 |
| Control retention and deletion of transcripts and recordings | GDPR Articles 5 and 17; CCPA deletion rights; NYC proposal (if adopted); litigation holds | In force (GDPR, CCPA); proposal pending | Operations and legal | Q3 |
| Limit agent actions that move money or change accounts | Regulation E; UDAAP; card-network rules | In force | Product and payments | Q8 |
| Contract for no training or secondary use of customer data | GDPR Article 28; state privacy laws; contract terms | In force | Procurement and legal | Q9 |
| Do not present the bot as a licensed professional where a state bars it | State laws (Section 1.2) | Varies by state | Design | G-series |
| Do not infer emotion from employees in EU workplaces | EU AI Act Article 5(1)(f) (Section 1.12) | In force since February 2, 2025 | Design and workforce management | Q10 |

# Part 1. Laws and regulations

## 1.1 US local laws

City and county rules are few but can carry their own enforcement. Most local AI activity is internal government policy (procurement rules, AI registers) rather than law that binds private virtual agents.

| Jurisdiction and law | Status | What it requires | Relevance to virtual agents |
|---|---|---|---|
| **New York City Local Law 144 (automated employment decision tools)** | In force since July 5, 2023 | Employers and agencies using automated tools to screen candidates or employees for NYC jobs must obtain an annual independent bias audit, publish a summary and give notice to candidates; enforcement by the city's consumer protection department (DCWP); no private right of action. A December 2025 New York State Comptroller audit found enforcement ineffective (for example, most related 311 calls were misrouted and the Comptroller found 17 potential violations where DCWP found one); DCWP committed to more proactive enforcement | Recruiting and HR virtual agents that screen, rank or schedule candidates |
| **New York City DCWP proposed rule on automated customer service** | Pending (proposed October 20, 2026; see 1.9.3) | Disclosure at the start, in the language used, that the consumer is dealing with an automated system; one-touch access to a human; reported three-year machine-readable interaction logs | Every chatbot and phone voice-response system serving NYC consumers; definition covers any channel where substantially all communication is automated, so chat and SMS are included |
| **New York City biometric identifier law (NYC Admin. Code 22-1201 and following)** | In force since 2021 (verify) | Commercial establishments that collect biometric identifiers must post notice and may not sell them; private right of action | Voice or face identification used at physical locations, kiosks or drive-throughs |
| **Portland, Oregon ban on private-entity facial recognition in places of public accommodation** | In force since 2021 (verify) | Prohibits private businesses from using face recognition in places open to the public | Kiosk, retail and drive-through agents with cameras |
| **City and county bans on government facial recognition (for example San Francisco, Boston, King County)** | In force (verify each) | Restrict government use; do not bind private businesses | Government virtual agents and public-sector deployments |
| **Municipal AI policies (for example Seattle, San Jose, Boston, New York City)** | Guidance | Procurement, transparency and AI-register requirements for city use | Vendors selling virtual agents to cities must meet them by contract |

### Penalties: US local laws

| Law | Maximum penalties | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| NYC Local Law 144 | USD 500 for a first violation; USD 500 to 1,500 for each later violation; each day an unaudited tool is used can count separately | Enforced by DCWP; no private right of action |
| NYC biometric identifier law | Private damages per violation (reported USD 500, or USD 5,000 for intentional sale violations; verify) | Private right of action |
| Portland facial recognition ban | Private damages per day of violation (amount verify) | Private right of action |
| NYC proposed automated customer-service rule | Proposed 6 RCNY 6-91: USD 525 for a first violation, USD 1,050 for a second and USD 3,500 for a third and later violation, each per violation; knowing violations of Admin. Code 20-703(d): USD 3,500 (T1: notice of hearing, October 20, 2026) | Pending |

## 1.2 US state laws

The table lists enacted laws and significant pending bills that bear directly on AI or virtual agents. Section 1.3 lists categories that exist in many states (privacy, recording, biometrics, insurance, deepfakes).

| State | Law | Status | What it requires |
|---|---|---|---|
| **California** | BOT Act (SB 1001, Bus. & Prof. Code 17940 and following) | In force since 2019 | Disclose a bot used to incentivize a sale or influence a vote, where a person could be misled |
| California | SB 243 (companion chatbots) | In force January 1, 2026 | Disclosure that a companion chatbot is AI; self-harm and suicide protocols; protections for minors |
| California | **AB 1609, Right to Human Customer Service Act** | Enacted, not yet effective: chaptered September 28, 2026 (Chapter 733, Statutes of 2026); effective January 1, 2027 per secondary sources (verify) | For large private businesses (as defined; secondary sources report more than USD 500 million in annual revenue): may not present a customer-service chatbot as a human; give the required disclosure where a reasonable person would be misled; offer a customer-service feature for reaching an agent during business hours; make a good-faith effort to connect the customer to an agent within 15 minutes or schedule an appointment; make a good-faith effort to limit initial and cumulative phone hold times (secondary sources give 15 minutes and one hour); post contact information on certain websites; public-prosecutor enforcement, up to USD 5,000 for a first violation and USD 10,000 for each later violation. Exemptions reported for regulated public-utility services, exclusive hospital lines and consumer reporting agencies. [T2: Digital Democracy bill digest, version of September 28, 2026.] The chaptered text was not retrieved; the hold-time figures and the revenue threshold come from secondary sources only |
| California | AI Transparency Act (SB 942 as amended by AB 853) | In force August 2, 2026; added duties for large platforms January 1, 2027 | Covered generative-AI providers must offer detection tools and provenance disclosures |
| California | CCPA regulations on automated decision-making, risk assessments and cybersecurity audits | In force January 1, 2026; ADMT compliance from January 1, 2027 | Pre-use notice, opt-out and access rights for significant automated decisions; risk assessments (existing activities by December 31, 2027); audits phased by revenue |
| California | SB 53 (Transparency in Frontier AI Act); AB 2013 (training-data transparency) | In force January 1, 2026 | Frontier developers publish safety frameworks and report incidents; generative-AI developers document training data |
| California | 2026 session: SB 947 (automated decision systems in employment, reported to bar AI-only firing decisions) and other bills on companion and customer-service chatbots, health-care AI (AB 1979, SB 503) and digital replicas; vetoes reported for AB 2575 and SB 903 | Enacted (effective dates verify; sources conflict) | Workplace, health-care and customer-service obligations |
| California | Invasion of Privacy Act (CIPA), Penal Code 631 and 632 | In force | All-party consent to record or eavesdrop; USD 5,000 statutory damages per violation; used against AI chat and call-analytics vendors (Part 2.3) |
| California | SB 1120 (physicians make medical-necessity decisions in utilization review) | In force January 1, 2025 | AI may support but not replace clinician review of coverage denials |
| **Colorado** | Colorado AI Act (SB 24-205) | **Repealed or replaced** by SB 26-189 (Chapter 131, signed May 14, 2026; developer and deployer duties effective January 1, 2027 per the enrolled act, T1). **Enjoined or stayed:** a federal magistrate judge stayed enforcement on April 27, 2026 after the Attorney General stipulated, pending the xAI litigation, and the stay reaches successor bills. The effective date of SB 26-189 depends on that litigation (T2; verify docket) | Original high-risk AI duties (developer and deployer care, impact assessments) no longer apply as written |
| Colorado | SB 26-189 (automated decision-making transparency; replaces SB 24-205) | Enacted, not yet effective: Chapter 131, signed May 14, 2026 (T1); developer and deployer duties effective January 1, 2027; Attorney General rules on post-adverse-outcome disclosure due by January 1, 2027. The AG adopted the ADMT and chatbot rules on November 19, 2026; they are not final (formal AG opinion requested; final 20 days after publication) | Transparency and consumer rights for consequential automated decisions; Attorney General enforcement under the Consumer Protection Act with a 60-day cure period (cure period through 2030 per the enrolled act, verify); no new private right of action |
| Colorado | Chatbot Safety Act (HB 26-1263, Conversational Artificial Intelligence Service Operator Requirements) | Signed May 29, 2026 per the final fiscal note (the Attorney General's page gives July 1, 2026). General effective date August 12, 2026 unless a referendum petition is filed; no petition was found, so the general provisions are presumed in force (verify with the Secretary of State). Operator requirements effective January 1, 2027 (bill summary, T1) | Clear disclosure that a user is interacting with AI; operator requirements, including content moderation and data protection for minors (verify) |
| **Connecticut** | SB 5, Public Act 26-15 ("An act concerning online safety"); broader high-risk AI bill (SB 2) never enacted | Enacted May 27, 2026; most sections effective October 1, 2026; AI companion duties (secs. 4 to 6) from January 1, 2027; employment-technology notices for technology deployed from October 1, 2027 | AI companion safety duties and employment-technology notices; Attorney General enforcement only, 60-day cure through December 31, 2027 |
| **Florida** | Telephone Solicitation Act (FTSA) and Security of Communications Act | In force | Mini-TCPA rules for automated calls and texts; all-party consent to record |
| **Georgia** | SB 540 (2026 chatbot law); SB 444 (AI in utilization review) | SB 540 enacted May 11, 2026, effective July 1, 2027; SB 444 effective January 1, 2027 | For minor account holders: AI disclosure, sexual-content measures, parental tools and a crisis protocol |
| **Hawaii** | SB 3001, Act 248 (Artificial Intelligence Disclosure and Safety Act) | In force (signed July 14, 2026, effective immediately; single source, verify) | AI companion operators must give clear notice that the companion is AI where a reasonable person could think it is human: a persistent visible disclaimer, or notice at the start of each session and at least once an hour; added protections for minors |
| **Idaho** | S 1297 (2026 conversational AI law) | Enacted April 1, 2026; effective July 1, 2027 (verify) | Tracker summary says customer-service and business-operations chatbots are exempt and liability falls on third-party operators of publicly available conversational AI (verify against enrolled text) |
| **Illinois** | Biometric Information Privacy Act (BIPA) | In force | Written notice and consent before collecting voiceprints or face geometry; retention schedule; private right of action (2024 amendment limits damages to one recovery per person, verify) |
| Illinois | HB 3773 (Human Rights Act amendment on AI in employment) | In force January 1, 2026 | No discriminatory AI use in employment decisions; notice when AI is used |
| Illinois | Artificial Intelligence Video Interview Act | In force since 2020 | Notice, explanation and consent before AI analysis of video interviews; deletion on request |
| Illinois | Wellness and Oversight for Psychological Resources Act (AI therapy limits) | In force 2025 (verify) | Restricts AI from providing therapy or presenting as a licensed therapist |
| Illinois | Frontier AI safety law (third state after California and New York) | Enacted (effective date verify) | Frontier developer duties |
| Illinois | Bot-disclosure bills: HB 3021 and SB 317 (Consumer AI Notice Act for customer-service chat); earlier HB 2435 | Pending: HB 3021 in House Rules; SB 317 passed the Senate only; no enacted Illinois general bot-disclosure law found as of November 2026 | Would require disclosure when consumers chat with AI in customer service |
| **Iowa** | SF 2417 (2026 chatbot law); HF 2635 (AI in utilization review) | SF 2417 enacted May 2, 2026, in force July 1, 2026; HF 2635 enacted May 13, 2026 | Operators must disclose AI to minor account holders persistently or at the start and every 3 hours |
| **Louisiana** | Data Privacy Act (2026) | Enacted, not yet effective (effective January 1, 2027; governor and signing date not verified) | Comprehensive privacy law with a cure period (T2; primary text not retrieved) |
| **Louisiana** | HB 686 (disclosure at the start of AI-made calls); HB 639 (AI in political calls) | Pending (status unclear) | AI-voice call disclosure |
| **Maine** | LD 1727 (AI chatbots in trade); LD 2082 (2026, AI therapy) | LD 1727 enacted June 12, 2025 (effective about 90 days after adjournment; verify) | Unfair trade practice to use an AI chatbot that could mislead a consumer into thinking they are dealing with a human, unless disclosed |
| **Maryland** | Mini-TCPA amendments (2024); facial-recognition-in-interviews law (2020); Wiretap Act | In force | Automated-call rules; consent before facial recognition in job interviews; all-party consent to record |
| **Maryland** | Protection From Predatory Pricing Act (HB 895) | In force October 1, 2026 | Grocery stores of at least 15,000 square feet and grocery delivery apps may not charge individualized or localized prices based on personal data; attorney-general enforcement, up to USD 10,000 for a first violation |
| **Michigan** | SB 760 (chatbot safety) | Pending, not law: passed the Senate 20-17 on April 29, 2026; referred to the House Communications and Technology Committee; no House action found after May 2026 (T2; check legislature.mi.gov) | Chatbot safety: keeps minors from AI companion chatbots deemed capable of encouraging self-harm, illegal activity or sexually explicit content (bill summary) |
| **Nebraska** | LB 525 (Conversational Artificial Intelligence Safety Act, combined with the Agricultural Data Privacy Act) | Enacted April 17, 2026; effective date verify (Nebraska default is three months after adjournment); stand-alone bill LB 1185 died | Conversational AI safety duties (verify details) |
| **Nevada** | Limits on AI in mental-health care (2025) | In force (verify) | Restricts AI representing itself as a mental-health provider |
| **New Jersey** | Mini-TCPA law (2024) | In force | Automated-call and text rules |
| **New York** | S9051-B / A10379-C (unsafe companion features for minors), chapter 368 of 2026, General Business Law sections 1800 to 1805 | Enacted (signed October 28, 2026), effective January 1, 2027 | Bars 12 categories of unsafe companion features for minors, including pretending to be human, encouraging self-harm and sexual content; age assurance required before such features stay on; AG-only enforcement with civil penalties up to USD 25,000 per violation (T1: signed October 28, 2026, chapter 368); attorney general may add features |
| New York | AI companion safeguards law, General Business Law article 47 (sections 1700 to 1704); Safe by Design Act (chapter 58 of 2026, part Y) | Article 47 in force November 5, 2025; Safe by Design Act effective January 1, 2027 | Crisis-detection protocols and periodic disclosure for AI companions; Attorney General penalties up to USD 15,000 per day |
| New York | RAISE Act (frontier models): original chapter 699 of 2025 repealed and replaced by S8828 (chapter 96 of 2026, signed March 27, 2026) | Enacted, effective January 1, 2027 | Frontier developer safety frameworks and incident reporting; DFS oversight (descriptions of the 2025 text are out of date) |
| New York | Algorithmic pricing disclosure; synthetic performer disclosure in advertising | In force (verify dates) | Disclose personalized algorithmic pricing; disclose synthetic performers in ads |
| New York | AB 2593 (consent for artificial or prerecorded voice robocalls) | Pending (introduced 2025) | AI-voice call consent |
| **Oklahoma** | Telephone Solicitation Act | In force | Mini-TCPA rules |
| Oklahoma | Consumer Data Privacy Act (SB 546, signed March 20, 2026) | Enacted, not yet effective (effective January 1, 2027) | Comprehensive privacy law; AG-only enforcement with a 30-day cure (T2; statute text not retrieved) |
| **Oregon** | HB 3865 (telephone solicitation) | In force January 1, 2026 | Covers texts; opt-out mechanisms and disclosures for automatic dialing and artificial-voice calls; reported limit of 3 solicitation calls per 24 hours (verify enacted text) |
| Oregon | SB 1546 (companion chatbots, Chapter 85, Laws of 2026); HB 2748 (AI may not use nursing titles) | Enacted April 6, 2026; effective January 1, 2027 (SB 1546). HB 2748 status to verify | Companion-chatbot safety; a user who shows ascertainable harm may sue for damages and injunctive relief; annual public report of crisis referrals (enrolled summary, T1). The USD 1,000 figure in earlier summaries is not in the enrolled summary; no AI posing as a nurse |
| **Pennsylvania** | SB 1090 (SAFECHAT Act) and HB 2006 (AI Companion Safety Act); enacted: Act 35 of 2025 (digital forgery) and Act 99 of 2026 (SB 806, AI deceptive advertising) | SB 1090 passed the Senate 49-1 and HB 2006 the House 133-70; neither is law and both die November 30, 2026 (HB 2006 passed the House 133-70 on September 28 and was referred to a Senate committee on October 13); Act 99 in force about December 21, 2026 | Companion-chatbot safety (bills); deceptive AI advertising (Act 99) |
| **Rhode Island** | HB 7350 (AI companion models); HB 7349 (AI in mental health care); HB 7538 (notice of AI visit documentation) | Enacted June 22, 2026; HB 7350 effective January 1, 2027; annual reports to the Attorney General from July 1, 2027 | Crisis protocols; not-human notice at the start of a session and every 3 hours |
| **Tennessee** | ELVIS Act (2024); 2026: SB 1580 (AI may not be represented as a qualified mental health professional), SB 837 (AI is not a "person"), HB 1513 (political deepfake disclaimer), SB 1493 and SB 1700 (studies) | ELVIS Act in force; SB 1580 in force July 1, 2026; no standalone 2026 companion-chatbot statute found | Protects voice and likeness against unauthorized AI cloning; bots must not claim to be licensed mental-health professionals |

See "Utah: all AI laws and bills" below |

Customer-service and business bots are excluded unless they sustain relationships and elicit emotional responses. No medical-professional disclosure found in the enrolled text |
| Washington | Biometric identifiers law (RCW 19.375); My Health My Data Act | In force | Biometric notice and consent; consumer health data consent with a private right of action |

**Volume indicator:** trackers reported 1,561 AI-related bills in 45 states by March 2026 and laws passed or enacted in 30 states between January 1 and October 15, 2026, including 14 chatbot-safety laws in the first half of the year.

### Utah: all AI laws and bills (2024 to 2026 sessions)

Utah has no single comprehensive AI act. It has several targeted chapters with different dates, and many summaries still describe the 2024 version of the AI Policy Act. **Important correction:** the July 1, 2027 repeal (Utah Code 63I-2-213) covers only Chapter 13-72 (the Office of AI Policy, the learning laboratory and sandbox agreements). The consumer disclosure duties in Chapter 13-77, the mental-health chatbot rules in Chapter 13-72a and the HB 276 chapters are **not** on the repeal list. On September 16, 2026 the Business and Labor Interim Committee voted 15-0-5 to recommend extending Chapter 13-72 to July 1, 2037; that is a recommendation, not law, so watch the 2027 General Session.

**Enacted and in force or scheduled**

| Law | Utah Code | Status | Requirements relevant to virtual agents | Penalties |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **SB 149 (2024), AI Policy Act**, amended by **SB 332 (2025)** and **HB 320 (2026)** | Ch. 13-72 | In force; Chapter 13-72 repealed July 1, 2027 unless extended | Creates the Office of AI Policy and the AI Learning Laboratory ("AI sandbox"). Regulatory mitigation agreements and, since HB 320 (effective May 6, 2026), joint interpretation agreements give participants temporary relief, with safeguards, disclosures and reporting. Agreements so far are mostly health (for example prescription-renewal and mental-health pilots) | Agreement terms; participants have no property right to participate |
| **SB 226 (2025), AI Consumer Protection Amendments**; enforcement confirmed by **SB 38 (2026)** | Ch. 13-77 | In force since May 7, 2025 (SB 38 enacted March 17, 2026) | **Generative AI** means a system trained on data, designed to simulate human conversation with a consumer by text, audio or visual means, that produces non-scripted, human-like outputs with limited or no human oversight (scripted IVR trees likely fall outside). **Consumer transactions:** disclose that the person is talking to generative AI "and not a human" when the person clearly and unambiguously asks. **Regulated occupations** (Department of Commerce licensed or certified): prominently disclose generative AI in "high-risk" interactions, verbally at the start of a voice interaction and in writing before a written one. High-risk means collecting sensitive data (health, financial, biometric) or giving personalized advice that may be relied on for significant financial, legal, medical or mental-health decisions. **Safe harbor** (13-77-104): no enforcement if the AI clearly and conspicuously discloses at the outset **and throughout** the interaction that it is AI and not human. **No AI defense** (13-77-102): "the AI said it" is not a defense to any consumer-protection violation | Division of Consumer Protection: administrative or court fine up to **$2,500 per violation**; up to **$5,000 per violation of an administrative or court order**; injunctions, disgorgement, restitution, fees and costs. No private action in the chapter, but a violation is also a violation of the Consumer Sales Practices Act (13-11-4), which has a consumer action section (untested) |
| **HB 452 (2025), mental-health chatbots** | Ch. 13-72a; 58-60-118 | In force since May 7, 2025 | Covers generative AI that a supplier represents, or a reasonable person would believe, provides mental-health therapy or helps manage conditions (scripted tools and pure referral tools excluded). Disclose that it is AI and not human before access, at the start of any interaction after seven days without use, and whenever asked. No sale or sharing of Utah users' health information or user input with third parties (narrow exceptions). Ads must be labeled; user input may not be used to target or customize ads. A licensing-law affirmative defense requires a written policy filed with the Division of Professional Licensing | Division of Consumer Protection: up to **$2,500 per violation**; up to **$5,000 per order violation**; no private right of action |
| **SB 180 (2025), police use of generative AI** | 53-25-901, 902 | In force since May 7, 2025 | Each law-enforcement agency must have a generative AI policy; reports drafted wholly or partly with generative AI need a disclaimer and the author's certification of review | Agency discipline; evidentiary consequences |
| **SB 271 (2025)** and **SB 256 (2026)**, AI impersonation and identity | Abuse of personal identity; defamation | In force (SB 256 effective May 6, 2026) | No commercial use of a person's simulated or recreated identity (including voice clones) in advertising, fundraising or solicitation without consent; no distributing tools whose primary purpose is unauthorized identity replication; written notice before suing for defamation over digitally created content | Civil remedies under the identity statute |
| **HB 289 (2026)**, AI-generated child sexual abuse material | Criminal code | In force since May 6, 2026 | New offenses for "apparent" (AI-generated) CSAM | Criminal |
| **HJR 26 (2026)**, Utah Rule of Evidence 707 | Rules of Evidence | Adopted (two-thirds vote, March 6, 2026) | Machine-generated evidence offered without an expert must meet reliability standards similar to Rule 702 | Admissibility |
| **SB 292 (2026)**, automated driving systems | Product liability | In force since May 6, 2026 | Level 4 and 5 developers liable only under product-liability rules; non-economic damages capped at $1,000,000 (not wrongful death); no punitive damages | Liability limits |
| **HB 273 (2026)**, classroom technology | Education | Enacted March 18, 2026 | Local education agencies must adopt technology and AI use policies by July 1, 2027; state board to publish model policies | Administrative |
| **HB 276 (2026)**, Digital Voyeurism Prevention Act and **Digital Content Provenance Standards Act** | Chs. 13-72b, 13-72c; 63A-16-215 | Signed March 24, 2026; **effective January 1, 2027**; capture-device duty for devices produced for sale from January 1, 2028 | **Covered providers** (publicly accessible generative AI with more than 1,000,000 monthly users in Utah, excluding internal-only systems) must embed a **latent disclosure** (provenance data with time, date and origin) in image, video and **audio** they create or substantially modify, so AI voice output is in scope. **Large online platforms** (more than 2,000,000 monthly users) must detect and display provenance data and not strip it. Generation services need verified consent before producing intimate images of identifiable people; platforms must remove counterfeit intimate images within 48 hours of notice; consent records kept 7 years | Provenance: Division of Consumer Protection, up to **$2,500 per violation** and **$5,000 per order violation**, disgorgement, injunctions. Intimate images: private right of action |
| **SB 319 (2026)**, health insurance preauthorization | 31A-22-650 | Signed March 19, 2026; **effective January 1, 2027** | Insurers must disclose on their website, and to the Insurance Department, in-network providers and enrollees, whether they use AI in authorization reviews; decisions within 7 days (72 hours urgent); adverse medical-necessity decisions must be made by a qualified individual using independent judgment and not relying solely on other sources (including AI) | Insurance Department enforcement |
| **Executive Order 2026-08**, Pro-Human AI principles | Governor's order | In effect since October 6, 2026 | Covered executive-branch agencies must avoid AI that does not follow the state's Pro-Human AI principles, train staff in AI literacy, document AI used in processes affecting the public and keep human review for decisions affecting individual rights (excludes the Attorney General, legislature, courts, higher education and some others) | Internal; affects state procurement of virtual agents |
| **SB 260 (2025) and SB 275 (2026), State-Endorsed Digital Identity (SEDI)** | Title 63A, Ch. 20 | SB 275 effective May 6, 2026; mDL sunset January 1, 2027 | Voluntary, wallet-based, selective-disclosure digital identity; no state tracking; Duty of Loyalty on verifiers and relying parties (details below) | Attorney general civil actions; no private right of action |
| Utah wiretap law (77-23a-4) | Criminal procedure | In force | One-party consent for a participant to record (relevant to AI note-takers and call recording). For calls with a participant in an all-party state, sources conflict: one says the stricter state rule usually controls, another says courts are divided. The statute text was not retrieved (T2; verify with counsel) | Criminal and civil |

**Utah State-Endorsed Digital Identity (SEDI): identity verification rules**

Utah's SEDI program is a **framework, not a single identity vendor**. The state endorses credentials that meet statutory rules rather than having one company verify everyone and act as the login gate, as ID.me does for the IRS and many state agencies (background). Utah's Chief Privacy Officer has publicly argued against centralized, third-party-gated identity models (reported). The design is meant to be open, multi-issuer and user-controlled.

| Item | Detail |
|---|---|
| **SB 260 (2025), Individual Digital Identity Amendments** | Signed March 27, 2025. The state does not establish a person's identity; it **endorses** digital identities that follow state rules. The Department of Government Operations, with the Chief Privacy Officer and Chief Information Officer, develops the framework. A state-endorsed identity must be controlled by the individual |
| **SB 275 (2026), State-Endorsed Digital Identity Act** (Utah Code Title 63A, Chapter 20) | Passed unanimously; **effective May 6, 2026**. Creates the SEDI program. The first SEDI credential had not yet been issued as of September 2026, and official implementation standards were not yet published (a working implementation guide, version 0.1.0-draft, May 2026, is not authoritative) |
| How it works | Wallet-based: residents who opt in hold a credential on their phone and present it online or offline. The personal digital identifier is created by or for the individual, cryptographically provable to be under the holder's control and portable between conformant wallets. Open, royalty-free standards required (for example mdoc, W3C Verifiable Credentials or SD-JWT could qualify) |
| Digital Identity Bill of Rights (63A-20-101) | Eleven rights, including: a physical ID option and freedom from compelled digitization; **selective disclosure** (prove one fact, such as being over 21, without revealing name, address or birth date); format neutrality (the same services whatever lawful ID format is used); freedom from state surveillance, profiling or tracking of ID presentations; no required device surrender |
| "No phone home" (63A-20-301(3)) | The credential may not include any mechanism that lets the department monitor or track where it is presented |
| Revocation | Only if compromised, issued in error or on fraudulent information, or at the holder's request |
| Enrollment data | Used only to issue and manage the credential or as the individual authorizes; kept only as long as needed; held in a state-controlled data center in Utah; disclosed only to the individual or under a warrant or court order |
| Wallet providers (63A-20-401) | Must support selective disclosure and age-over checks without revealing age or birth date; keep a presentation log that only the holder can see, export and delete; process attributes only when needed for a presentation, with conspicuous notice and consent |
| **Duty of Loyalty** (63A-20-701) | Binds the department, wallet providers, **verifiers, relying parties** and digital guardians: no processing of identity attributes that conflicts with the individual's best interests, exploits them, creates disproportionate risk or causes harm. This applies whether or not the person consented |
| Government entities (63A-20-304) | May not reward digital over physical ID, withhold services from people using physical ID, or require device surrender. New government systems that accept digital identity must accept SEDI within three months of the first SEDI being issued (or have a plan if not technically feasible) |
| Health care providers (63A-20-305) | Providers receiving at least $10 million a year in public funds, and running a program that accepts digital identity, must accept SEDI within two years of the first issuance |
| Mobile driver license | Utah's existing mDL (Utah Code 53-3-235) sunsets **January 1, 2027**; SEDI replaces it rather than running in parallel |
| Oversight | Annual department reports to the legislature from January 1, 2027 (metrics, security incidents, wallet and verifier ecosystem); Legislative Auditor General audit starting January 1, 2028, report by October 31, 2028 |
| Enforcement and penalties | Complaints go to the state data privacy ombudsperson, who can refer them to the **attorney general**. The attorney general can issue civil investigative demands and sue for injunctive and declaratory relief, restitution, disgorgement, actual damages, costs and attorney fees. **No private right of action** and no fixed per-violation fine found (verify against the enrolled text). Critics (ACLU) note the law does not stop private businesses from demanding digital ID |

**What SEDI means for a virtual agent.** (1) An agent that verifies identity for a Utah government service must always offer a path that works with physical ID and may not give digital-ID users better service. (2) A business whose agent requests SEDI attributes acts as a verifier or relying party: request only the attributes needed, state the purpose, prefer yes-or-no proofs (for example "over 21") and do not keep, profile or reuse the attributes, given the Duty of Loyalty. (3) Do not ask callers to send screenshots of or hand over their device. (4) Agencies and large publicly funded health providers that already accept digital identity must plan SEDI acceptance on the statutory clocks. (5) Utah's model is a policy alternative to single-vendor identity gates such as ID.me; agencies that use those gates for federal programs (for example IRS online accounts) still follow the federal requirements (NIST SP 800-63 identity assurance; background).

**2026 bills that did not pass (possible 2027 refiling)**

| Bill | What it would have done | Outcome |
|---|---|---|
| **HB 286, AI Transparency Act** | Large frontier developers (models trained with at least 10^26 operations, revenue of at least $500 million) to publish public-safety and child-protection plans and risk assessments, report safety incidents, protect whistleblowers; civil penalties up to $1 million for a first violation and $3 million after | Committee 8-0-2; enacting clause struck March 6, 2026 (reports that the White House "blocked" it are disputed) |
| **HB 438, Companion Chatbot Safety Act** | Companion-chatbot operators to follow the Utah Consumer Privacy Act, crisis-response duties for suicide and self-harm, extra protections and parental controls for minors, advertising limits, annual reports to the Office of AI Policy; Division enforcement (one draft also had $1,000 statutory damages) | Passed House 68-1-6; **failed in the Senate 12-15-2 on March 5, 2026**; enacting clause struck March 6. Earlier reports that it passed the Senate 26-1 were wrong |
| **SB 205**, police AI amendments | Agencies to post their generative AI policies publicly | Passed Senate 20-0-9; died in the House |
| **SB 322**, educational technology sandbox | Time-limited AI pilots in schools with red-teaming, parental opt-out and independent evaluation | Did not pass (verify) |
| **SB 177**, algorithmic pricing disclosure | Label prices set by algorithms using personal data; fines up to $2,500 | Died |
| **HB 504**, AI in political ads | Disclosure on printed political ads with AI-generated visuals; up to $1,000 per violation | Died in committee |
| **HB 606**, surveillance technology | Statutory authorization before police use of surveillance or investigative technology; private right of action | Introduced only |

**What Utah means for a virtual agent:** answer "Am I talking to a bot?" truthfully every time; for licensed-profession, health, financial or legal use, disclose at the start and keep the disclosure visible or repeated throughout (the safe harbor needs both); never use user input from a mental-health conversation for ads or share it; from January 1, 2027, large public generative AI providers must embed provenance data in AI audio, images and video; and plan for a companion-chatbot bill in 2027.

### Penalties: US state laws

Amounts are statutory maximums unless noted; many are counted per violation, per consumer or per day, so exposure scales with volume.

| State and law | Maximum penalties | Who enforces |
|---|---|---|
| California CCPA and ADMT regulations | USD 2,663 per violation; USD 7,988 per intentional violation or violation involving consumers under 16 (amounts in effect since January 1, 2025; next inflation adjustment January 1, 2027) | CPPA and Attorney General |
| California BOT Act | Civil penalties under the Unfair Competition Law (background: up to USD 2,500 per violation; verify) | Attorney General, district attorneys |
| California SB 243 (companion chatbots) | Private action for injunctive relief, damages (reported as the greater of actual damages or USD 1,000 per violation; sources describe this differently) and attorney's fees | Private plaintiffs |
| California AB 1609 (human customer service) | USD 5,000 for a first violation; USD 10,000 for each later violation | Public prosecutors |
| California AI Transparency Act (SB 942) | USD 5,000 per violation; each day of noncompliance can be a separate violation | Attorney General, city attorneys, county counsel |
| California SB 53 (frontier AI) | Up to USD 1 million per violation (background; verify) | Attorney General |
| California Invasion of Privacy Act | USD 5,000 per violation or three times actual damages | Private plaintiffs |
| Colorado AI laws (SB 26-189, replacing SB 24-205) and Colorado consumer protection | Up to USD 20,000 per violation, counted for each affected consumer or transaction | Attorney General |
| Colorado HB 26-1263 (chatbots) and SB 26-189 (ADMT) | Consumer-protection penalties (amounts verify) | Attorney General |
| Illinois BIPA | USD 1,000 per negligent violation or USD 5,000 per intentional or reckless violation (or actual damages if greater); since 2024, one recovery per person rather than per scan (verify) | Private plaintiffs |
| Illinois HB 3773 (AI in employment) | Human Rights Act remedies: actual damages, back pay, attorney's fees and civil penalties | Department of Human Rights, courts |
| Maine LD 1727 | Unfair Trade Practices Act remedies (background: civil penalties up to USD 10,000 per violation; verify) | Attorney General |
| Maryland HB 895 (surveillance pricing) | Up to USD 10,000 for a first violation | Attorney General |
| New York S9051-B (companion features for minors) | Up to USD 25,000 per violation | Attorney General |
| New York AI companion law (GBL article 47) | Up to USD 15,000 per day | Attorney General |
| Connecticut PA 26-15 | AG enforcement; some duties reported at USD 1,000 per violation (verify) | Attorney General |
| New York RAISE Act | Up to USD 1 million for a first violation; up to USD 3 million for later violations | Attorney General |
| Texas TRAIGA | USD 10,000 to 12,000 per curable violation; USD 80,000 to 200,000 per uncurable violation; USD 2,000 to 40,000 per day for continuing violations; 60-day cure period | Attorney General only |
| Texas CUBI (biometrics) | Up to USD 25,000 per violation (background; verify) | Attorney General |
| Utah AI laws (Ch. 13-77 disclosure, Ch. 13-72a mental-health chatbots, Ch. 13-72c provenance from 2027) | Up to USD 2,500 per violation; up to USD 5,000 per violation of an administrative or court order; disgorgement and injunctions; HB 276 intimate-image chapter adds a private right of action | Division of Consumer Protection, courts |
| Washington HB 2225 (companion chatbots) | Consumer Protection Act: actual damages, discretionary trebling capped at USD 25,000, injunctions, attorney's fees (one source says no statutory damages; verify); Attorney General civil penalties under the Consumer Protection Act | Private plaintiffs and Attorney General |
| Florida Telephone Solicitation Act | USD 500 per violation, up to USD 1,500 if willful (background; verify) | Private plaintiffs |
| Other mini-TCPA laws (for example Oklahoma, Maryland, New Jersey, Texas) | Statutory damages per call or text, often USD 500 to 1,500, plus civil penalties (verify each) | Private plaintiffs, attorneys general |
| Comprehensive state privacy laws | Commonly up to USD 7,500 per violation (varies by state; verify) | Attorneys general |
| Hawaii Act 248 (AI companions) | Not confirmed | Verify |

## 1.3 US laws that cut across many states

| Category | States (verify current list) | What it means for virtual agents |
|---|---|---|
| **Comprehensive consumer privacy laws** (opt-outs from profiling for significant decisions, sensitive-data consent, data-protection assessments) | California, Colorado, Connecticut, Delaware, Indiana, Iowa, Kentucky, Maryland, Minnesota, Montana, Nebraska, New Hampshire, New Jersey, Oregon, Rhode Island, Tennessee, Texas, Utah, Virginia (the 2026 enactments in Alabama, Louisiana, Oklahoma and Vermont are in Section 1.2) | Notice, opt-out of profiling, assessments for high-risk processing, rights requests that reach transcripts and recordings |
| **All-party consent to record calls** | California, Florida, Illinois, Maryland, Massachusetts, Montana (sources conflict; see Section 1.2), New Hampshire, Pennsylvania, Washington, and others in some circumstances (for example Connecticut, Delaware, Nevada) | Notice and consent before recording or AI analysis; apply the stricter rule on interstate calls |
| **Biometric privacy laws** | Illinois (BIPA), Texas (CUBI), Washington; biometric provisions in several comprehensive privacy laws | Consent before voiceprints or face data; retention and deletion |
| **State "mini-TCPA" and telemarketing laws** | Florida, Oklahoma, Maryland, New Jersey, Oregon, Texas, Utah and others | Stricter calling hours, consent, frequency caps, registration and private rights of action |
| **State Do-Not-Call lists** | Many states | Scrub per state in addition to the national registry |
| **NAIC Model Bulletin on insurers' use of AI** (adopted as state bulletins) | About 24 to 25 states plus D.C. (NAIC map as of May 1, 2026) | Governance program, risk management, vendor oversight and documentation for insurer AI |
| **AI in health-insurance utilization review** | Several states (for example California SB 1120) | Licensed clinicians must make adverse medical-necessity decisions |
| **Election deepfake laws** | A majority of states | Disclosure or bans on deceptive AI election content near elections |
| **Non-consensual intimate deepfakes** | Nearly all states | Criminal and civil liability for AI sexual imagery |
| **Voice and likeness (right of publicity)** | Tennessee (ELVIS Act), California, New York and others | Consent before cloning a real person's voice or likeness |

## 1.4 US federal laws, regulations and executive actions

| Law, rule or action | Status | Relevance to virtual agents |
|---|---|---|
| **Telephone Consumer Protection Act (TCPA) and FCC Declaratory Ruling FCC 24-17 (February 2024)** | In force | AI-generated voices count as "artificial or prerecorded voice"; prior express consent for calls to wireless numbers, prior express written consent for telemarketing; 8 a.m. to 9 p.m. calling hours; Do-Not-Call; opt-out mechanism; USD 500 per violation, up to USD 1,500 if willful. Since *McLaughlin Chiropractic v. McKesson* (US Supreme Court, June 2025), district courts are not bound by FCC interpretations, so FCC 24-17 is persuasive rather than binding; the Fifth Circuit (*Bradford v. Sovereign Pest Control*, February 25, 2026) held the statute requires only prior express consent, oral or written, and rejected the FCC written-consent rule (other courts split) |
| FCC TCPA consent-revocation order, FCC 26-67 (adopted September 30, 2026; Federal Register October 26, 2026, Doc. 2026-21821) | In force November 25, 2026 (T1, Federal Register public-inspection text; CG Docket 02-278). Callers may designate one exclusive revocation method: an automated IVR or keypress on calls; the reply words STOP, QUIT, END, REVOKE, OPT OUT, CANCEL or UNSUBSCRIBE on texts; or a designated website or phone number. The method must be disclosed on the call or in the text. Callers that do not designate one must honor any reasonable means within 10 business days. Revoke-all was not adopted, and the January 31, 2027 date is superseded. Informational-call revocations may be limited to that category. A fraud-alert exemption for financial institutions is included (order summary). The companion further notice (Doc. 2026-21820) would shorten the honoring period and require revoke-all; comments due November 25, 2026, replies December 28, 2026 (T1) | Recognize the designated revocation method in calls and texts and route it to suppression; other reasonable revocations still need checking for non-designating callers |
| FCC one-to-one consent rule (2023) | Vacated by the Eleventh Circuit (January 2025) and later repealed | Pre-2023 written-consent standard governs |
| FCC AI-generated call NPRM (August 2024) | Pending; no adopted rule found | Would define AI-generated calls and require AI disclosure |
| STIR/SHAKEN caller-ID authentication rules | In force | Accurate, authenticated caller ID for outbound agents |
| **FTC Act Section 5** (unfair or deceptive practices) | In force | Deceptive bot claims, undisclosed AI, unsubstantiated "AI" marketing (Part 2.7) |
| FTC Government and Business Impersonation Rule | In force since April 2024; extension to individuals proposed | Bots may not impersonate governments, businesses or (if extended) individuals |
| Telemarketing Sales Rule | In force | Disclosures, misrepresentation bans, recordkeeping |
| **Children's Online Privacy Protection Act (COPPA) and amended Rule** | In force; amended Rule effective June 23, 2025, compliance by April 22, 2026; FTC policy statement of February 25, 2026 limits enforcement for data collected solely for age verification | Verifiable parental consent, retention limits and security program for child-directed agents |
| FTC proposed enforcement policy statement on personalized pricing | Proposed August 19, 2026 (comments closed September 25, 2026) | Undisclosed personalized pricing likely deceptive or unfair where shoppers expect a posted price; not a ban. New Jersey (grocery, from August 1, 2027) and Connecticut have related state laws (verify) |
| **TAKE IT DOWN Act (2025)** | In force; FTC enforcement of platform duties from May 19, 2026 (FTC complaint portal at TakeItDown.ftc.gov; FTC letters to 15 platforms in May 2026; T1) | Covered platforms must remove non-consensual intimate images, including AI-generated ones, within 48 hours of a valid request; penalties reported up to USD 53,088 per violation |
| CAN-SPAM Act | In force | Commercial email sent by agents |
| **Equal Credit Opportunity Act and Regulation B; Fair Credit Reporting Act** | In force | Specific adverse-action reasons even when AI is used (CFPB Circulars 2022-03 and 2023-03; current status after the CFPB's 2025 guidance review verify). CFPB withdrew Circulars 2022-03 and 2023-03 among 67 guidance documents in May 2025; its Regulation B rule (effective July 21, 2026) removes disparate-impact liability under ECOA, but disparate treatment, including through proxies, remains unlawful and specific adverse-action reasons are still required |
| Gramm-Leach-Bliley Act and FTC Safeguards Rule | In force | Security program for customer financial data |
| Fair Housing Act; Title VII; ADEA; ADA | In force | Discrimination through algorithms (Part 2.5); EEOC removed its AI technical assistance (including the 2022 ADA and AI guidance) in January 2025, but the statutes still apply (Section 3.13). HUD withdrew its AI fair-housing guidance (September 17, 2025) and proposed removing its disparate-impact rule (January 14, 2026); private rights of action are unchanged |
| **HIPAA Privacy, Security and Breach Notification Rules** | In force; Security Rule overhaul proposed January 2025, final status not confirmed | Business associate agreements, minimum necessary, safeguards, breach notice (Part 3.2) |
| **Section 1557 nondiscrimination rule, 45 CFR 92.210** | In force; compliance reported due May 1, 2025 | Reasonable efforts to identify and mitigate discrimination risk from patient-care decision-support tools |
| ONC HTI-1 rule, 45 CFR 170.315(b)(11) | In force | Certified health IT must support source attributes and risk management for evidence-based and predictive decision-support interventions |
| CMS Medicare Advantage rule (CMS-4201-F) and February 6, 2024 FAQ | In force | Algorithms may assist but coverage decisions must reflect individual circumstances; predictions alone cannot end post-acute care. The CY2026 Medicare Advantage final rule (April 2025) did not finalize proposed AI-use limits (T2) |
| FDA clinical decision support and general wellness guidance (January 2026) | Guidance | Which AI health functions are regulated devices |
| 42 CFR Part 2 | In force | Substance-use treatment records |
| Americans with Disabilities Act; DOJ Title II web rule; HHS Section 504 web rule | In force; DOJ interim final rule (April 20, 2026) moved Title II compliance to April 26, 2027 (50,000 or more people) and April 26, 2028 (smaller); HHS moved Section 504 compliance to May 11, 2027 and May 10, 2028; both at WCAG 2.1 AA | Accessible chat and voice channels (Section 3.13) |
| Section 508 of the Rehabilitation Act | In force | Accessibility for federal agencies and their vendors |
| **Executive Orders 14148 and 14179 (January 2025)** and **Executive Order 14365 (December 11, 2025)** | In force | EO 14148 revoked the 2023 AI executive order (EO 14110) and EO 14179 set a new pro-innovation AI policy (verify); EO 14365 directs a DOJ AI Litigation Task Force to challenge "onerous" state AI laws and ties some BEAD funding to state AI policy (Commerce's list not published as of October 27, 2026) |
| FTC AI-accuracy proposed policy statement (91 FR 41638, July 7, 2026) | Proposed; comments closed July 31, 2026 (T1). Says the FTC Act does not expressly preempt state law (implied preemption only to the extent of conflict); disclaimers must be prominent; hallucinations alone are not a Section 5 issue, but misstating their likelihood can be | None (proposal) |
| *Trump v. Slaughter*, No. 25-332 (June 29, 2026) | In force (T1). FTC commissioners are removable at will; the for-cause protection is contrary to separation of powers (6-3 per press reports). FTC AI and chatbot enforcement now follows presidential control | None directly |
| *FCC v. AT&T* (June 4, 2026) | In force (T1). FCC forfeiture orders under 47 U.S.C. 503(b)(4) do not violate the Seventh Amendment (no jury); scope to verify | None directly |
| OMB memoranda M-25-21 and M-25-22 (April 2025) | In force for federal agencies (verify) | AI use and procurement rules for agencies buying virtual agents |
| Copyright Act; Copyright Office AI reports (2025) | In force / guidance | Human authorship required (Part 2.8); training-data questions |
| **Pending federal bills** | Pending | GUARD Act (S.3062, passed Senate Judiciary 22-0 on April 30, 2026; would bar AI companions for minors and require non-human disclosure); House companion H.R.8623; FRONTIER Act (July 2026) and other preemption proposals; White House legislative framework sent to Congress March 20, 2026; DOJ AI Litigation Task Force established January 9, 2026 under EO 14365; FTC proposed policy statement (July 1, 2026) that state laws requiring deceptive AI outputs are preempted by the FTC Act (proposal only); Ninth Circuit argument in an xAI challenge to a state AI law scheduled November 18, 2026 (verify); **no preemption statute or court ruling as of mid-November 2026** |

### Federal bills with momentum (119th Congress)

Democrats won control of both the House and the Senate in the November 3, 2026 midterm elections ([IAPP, November 4, 2026](https://iapp.org/news/a/democratic-midterm-wins-set-stage-for-tougher-tech-and-ai-scrutiny)). Bills not enacted by January 3, 2027 lapse and must be reintroduced; a Democratic Congress is expected to revisit AI safety, automated decision-making, children's online safety and privacy, but still needs the President's signature or a veto-proof majority.

All bills below die on January 3, 2027 unless enacted in the lame-duck session. From January 2027, Democrats control both chambers, but any bill still needs the President's signature or a veto-proof vote.

| Kids Internet and Digital Safety (KIDS) Act, H.R. 7757 | House Energy and Commerce package | Passed House June 29, 2026, 267-117, under suspension of the rules. Pending in Senate. | Combines several bills, including KOSA without a duty of care (platforms must adopt policies, safeguards and parental tools), COPPA 2.0, SAFE BOTs, AWARE, SCREEN, and a minors' data-broker registry. Enforced by the FTC (civil penalties) and state AGs. Preempts only conflicting state laws and keeps state tort and product-liability law. | Medium (lame duck only). Senate KOSA sponsors object that the duty of care was removed. | [CRS LSB11465](https://www.everycrsreport.com/files/2026-08-03_LSB11465_ebecf6f50f79343032de7bd1819e3e9ab523ff82.html) |

### Penalties: US federal laws

| Law | Maximum penalties | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| TCPA | USD 500 per call or text; up to USD 1,500 if willful or knowing (private actions, no aggregate cap); FCC forfeitures assessed per call (for example USD 1,000 per call in the 2024 AI-voice robocall order) | Class actions drive exposure |
| FTC rules (Impersonation Rule, Telemarketing Sales Rule, COPPA, CAN-SPAM, TAKE IT DOWN Act notice-and-removal duties) | Up to USD 53,088 per violation (2025 level; the FTC kept it unchanged for 2026); each day of a continuing violation can count separately | Section 5 deception without a rule violation generally brings injunctions and redress rather than first-violation fines |
| HIPAA (amounts effective January 28, 2026) | Tier 1 (did not know): USD 145 to 73,011 per violation; Tier 2 (reasonable cause): USD 1,461 to 73,011; Tier 3 (willful neglect, corrected): USD 14,602 to 73,011; Tier 4 (willful neglect, not corrected): USD 73,011 to 2,190,294; calendar-year cap of USD 2,190,294 per identical provision (OCR's 2019 enforcement discretion applies lower caps to Tiers 1 to 3); criminal penalties also possible | Inflation-adjusted annually |
| ECOA / Regulation B | Actual damages; punitive damages up to USD 10,000 in individual actions and the lesser of USD 500,000 or 1% of net worth in class actions (background; verify) | Plus CFPB and prudential regulator enforcement |
| FCRA | USD 100 to 1,000 statutory damages per willful violation plus punitive damages (background; verify) | |
| Consumer Financial Protection Act (UDAAP) | Tiered daily civil penalties, inflation-adjusted (verify current amounts) | CFPB and state attorneys general |
| ADA | Title III: injunctive relief and fees in private suits; DOJ civil penalties up to USD 118,225 (first violation) and USD 236,451 (subsequent), the 2025 amounts kept for 2026; California Unruh Act adds USD 4,000 minimum statutory damages per violation (Section 3.13) | DOJ, private plaintiffs |
| SEC (AI washing) | Case-by-case penalties (for example USD 400,000 total in the 2024 Delphia and Global Predictions orders) | |

## 1.5 European Union

| Instrument | Status | Relevance to virtual agents |
|---|---|---|
| **AI Act (Regulation (EU) 2024/1689)** | In force in stages: prohibitions and AI literacy from February 2, 2025; general-purpose AI duties from August 2, 2025; **Article 50 transparency from August 2, 2026** | Tell users they are interacting with AI unless obvious; mark synthetic audio, images, video and text; label deepfakes; prohibited manipulative and exploitative practices; high-risk duties for uses such as credit, employment and essential services. Fines up to EUR 35 million or 7% of turnover for prohibited practices |
| Digital Omnibus on AI (Regulation (EU) 2026/1744) | In force July 27, 2026 (T1: EUR-Lex; regulation dated July 8, 2026) | Moves Annex III stand-alone high-risk obligations to **December 2, 2027** and Annex I product-embedded high-risk to **August 2, 2028** (secondary sources; article text not re-read). Article 50(1), (3) and (4) dates unchanged. Article 50(2) marking grace applies only to systems placed on the market before August 2, 2026, with compliance from **December 2, 2026** (T1 FAQ). New Article 5 prohibitions on non-consensual intimate imagery and CSAM generation apply from December 2, 2026 (scope from the Parliament press release, T1; date T2) |
| Code of Practice on marking and labelling AI-generated content | Guidance (final June 10, 2026; voluntary). The Commission (July 8) and the AI Board (July 9) found it adequate for Article 50(2), (4) and (5); about 190 signatories by end July (T1) | Practical route to Article 50 compliance; voluntary, so not binding in itself |
| Commission guidelines on Article 50 transparency obligations | Guidance, published July 20, 2026 (T1) | Disclosure from the first interaction (Article 50(1)); narrow 'obvious' exception; scope and carve-outs for Article 50(2). Sources differ on whether formal adoption awaits all language versions |
| AI Office enforcement of general-purpose AI duties | In force from August 2, 2026 (T1) | Information requests, model access, risk-mitigation orders, fines up to 3% of global turnover, restriction and recall. Compliance dialogues come first; no fine found. Article 50 is enforced mainly by national market-surveillance authorities (the AI Office only for GPAI-based systems of the same provider and for systems in VLOPs and VLOSEs); Article 50 fines up to EUR 15 million or 3% |
| EDPB Guidelines 03/2026 on web scraping for generative AI | Consultation closed July 2026; final not seen (T1) | Personal data scraped to train or operate generative AI (content not reviewed) |
| **GDPR (Regulation (EU) 2016/679)** | In force | Lawful basis, transparency, Article 22 automated decisions, DPIAs, data-subject rights, transfers, processor contracts; CJEU case law on automated scoring and explanations (Part 2.3) |
| Digital Omnibus on data (GDPR, ePrivacy and Data Act changes), procedure 2025/0360(COD) | Pending (Council mandate October 21, 2026; Parliament negotiations not expected before March 2027) | The Council text reinstates the GDPR Article 22 default prohibition on solely automated significant decisions (the Commission had proposed reframing it); says AI development is not itself a legitimate interest; shortens the cookie re-ask period to four months (T2, Addleshaw, October 26, 2026) |
| **ePrivacy Directive (2002/58/EC)** | In force | Consent for automated marketing calls and for cookies or SDKs in chat widgets |
| **Consumer Credit Directive (EU) 2023/2225 (CCD2)** | Applies November 20, 2026 (T1 header; articles not read) | Credit information and creditworthiness; check for any human-contact rights in credit products (verify) |
| **Distance Financial Services Directive (EU) 2023/2673** | Applies June 19, 2026 (T1 header) | Right to request human intervention on online financial contracts (Article 16(e), verify) |
| **Digital Services Act** | In force | Duties for platforms that host or recommend content, including generative features |
| **Consumer law: Unfair Commercial Practices Directive, Consumer Rights Directive** | In force | Misleading bot statements, dark patterns, cancellation friction |
| Digital Fairness Act | Pending: Commission proposal adopted November 11, 2026 with a Simplification Directive; a regulation that would replace the 2005 Unfair Commercial Practices Directive (Commission page, T1; proposal text not reviewed) | Dark patterns, addictive design, exploitative personalization, minors |
| **Consumer Credit Directive (EU) 2023/2225** | Applies from November 20, 2026 (transposition was due November 20, 2025; several states late) | Right to human intervention, explanation and review when creditworthiness assessment uses automated processing |
| **Distance Financial Services Directive (EU) 2023/2673** (folded into the Consumer Rights Directive) | Applies since June 19, 2026 | When robo-advice or chatbots are used to sell financial services at a distance, consumers can request and receive human intervention before the contract and, where justified, afterwards; withdrawal button for online contracts |
| **Platform Work Directive (EU) 2024/2831** | Transposition due December 2, 2026 | Transparency, human oversight and data limits for algorithmic management of platform workers |
| **European Accessibility Act (Directive (EU) 2019/882)** | Applies since June 28, 2025 | Accessible customer-service channels, including chatbots |
| **Product Liability Directive (EU) 2024/2853** | Transposition due December 9, 2026 | Software and AI are products; strict liability for defects, including missing security updates |
| AI Liability Directive | Withdrawn by the Commission in 2025 (verify) | No separate AI fault-liability regime |
| **Cyber Resilience Act (Regulation (EU) 2024/2847)** | Reporting obligations from September 11, 2026; most obligations from December 11, 2027 | Report actively exploited vulnerabilities and severe incidents through ENISA's Single Reporting Platform; fines up to EUR 15 million or 2.5% |
| **NIS2 Directive** | In force; national transposition continuing | Security and incident reporting for in-scope entities and suppliers |
| **DORA (Regulation (EU) 2022/2554)** | In force since January 17, 2025 | ICT risk and third-party oversight for financial entities using AI vendors |
| Data Act | In force since September 12, 2025 | Data access and cloud-switching terms |
| Copyright in the Digital Single Market Directive, Article 4 | In force | Text-and-data-mining opt-outs relevant to training and retrieval content |
| Medical Device and In Vitro Diagnostic Regulations | In force | AI that diagnoses or treats |

### Penalties: EU laws

| Instrument | Maximum fines | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| AI Act | Prohibited practices: EUR 35 million or 7% of worldwide annual turnover; most other obligations (including Article 50 transparency): EUR 15 million or 3%; supplying incorrect information: EUR 7.5 million or 1%; general-purpose AI model providers: EUR 15 million or 3% (imposed by the Commission) | Higher of the two amounts; for SMEs and start-ups the lower |
| GDPR | EUR 20 million or 4% of worldwide annual turnover; EUR 10 million or 2% for lesser infringements | Higher of the two |
| Digital Services Act | Up to 6% of worldwide annual turnover; periodic penalties up to 5% of average daily turnover | Commission for very large platforms; national coordinators otherwise |
| Digital Markets Act | Up to 10% of worldwide turnover, 20% for repeat infringements (background) | Gatekeepers only |
| Cyber Resilience Act | Essential requirements and reporting: EUR 15 million or 2.5%; other obligations: EUR 10 million or 2%; incorrect information: EUR 5 million or 1% (lower tiers background; verify) | Higher of the two |
| NIS2 Directive | Essential entities: at least EUR 10 million or 2%; important entities: at least EUR 7 million or 1.4% | Minimum maximums; member states may set higher |
| DORA | Set by member states; critical ICT third-party providers face Commission-level periodic penalties of up to 1% of average daily worldwide turnover (background; verify) | Financial sector |
| Consumer law (UCPD and Consumer Rights Directive, as amended by the Omnibus Directive) | For widespread infringements, at least 4% of annual turnover in the member states concerned, or EUR 2 million where turnover data is unavailable (background; verify) | Coordinated enforcement under the CPC Regulation |
| Consumer Credit Directive 2; Distance Financial Services Directive; European Accessibility Act; Data Act | Effective, proportionate and dissuasive penalties set by member states | National amounts vary |
| Product Liability Directive | No fines; strict liability for compensation | Damages claims |

| **Ireland** | European Union (Distance Contracts for Financial Services) Regulations 2026; Artificial Intelligence Companion Services (Protection of Children) Bill 2026 (Bill 93 of 2026) | Regulations signed mid-2026 (reported as June 30 or July 3); Bill introduced September 29, 2026 | Right to speak to a person instead of a chatbot when buying financial products online or by phone, enforced by the CCPC and the Central Bank; proposed design and disclosure rules for AI companions used by children |

| **Spain** | Draft AI law with labeling sanctions and the AESIA supervisor | Pending (verify) | Labeling of AI-generated content |
| **All member states** | National call-recording, telemarketing-register, employee-monitoring and works-council rules; national transposition of the CCD2, PWD, PLD and NIS2 | Varies | Check each country of operation |

### Penalties: EU member-state laws

| Member state | Penalties | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| Italy (Law 132/2025) | Criminal offense for unlawful dissemination of harmful AI deepfakes (reported 1 to 5 years' imprisonment; verify); aggravating circumstances for crimes committed with AI | National AI law |
| Germany | Accessibility (BFSG) fines up to EUR 100,000 (background; verify); unauthorized recording of private speech is a criminal offense (up to 3 years under StGB section 201, background) | |
| Ireland | Offences under the distance financial services regulations prosecuted by the CCPC or Central Bank (amounts verify) | |
| All member states | GDPR, AI Act, NIS2 and consumer-law fines enforced by national authorities | National authorities designated under the AI Act |
