| Law or regime | Status | Relevance to virtual agents |
|---|---|---|
| **UK GDPR and Data Protection Act 2018, as amended by the Data (Use and Access) Act 2025** | Main provisions in force February 5, 2026 (SI 2026/82; PECR enforcement schedule also in force); complaints-procedure duty from June 19, 2026 (T1) | Articles 22A to 22D inserted by section 80 (T1): a 'meaningful human involvement' test, special-category limits, safeguards (information, representations, human intervention, contest) and a regulation power; lawful basis; DPIAs; data-subject rights |
| **Privacy and Electronic Communications Regulations (PECR)**, as amended | In force; fines now up to GBP 17.5 million or 4% of turnover | Prior consent for automated marketing calls; TPS and Corporate TPS screening for live calls; caller identification; consent for cookies and SDKs (certain low-risk cookies exempt) |
| **Online Safety Act 2023** | In force in phases | Chatbots in scope where they enable user-to-user sharing, search or pornographic content (Ofcom guidance, December 2025); highly effective age assurance where required; government pledged on February 15, 2026 to bring all AI chatbots in scope (pending) |
| **Digital Markets, Competition and Consumers Act 2024** | Consumer-enforcement regime in force since April 2025 | Misleading AI statements, subscription traps and dark patterns; CMA fines reported up to 10% of global turnover (verify) |
| Consumer Rights Act 2015 | In force | Services must be provided with reasonable care; statements bind the trader |
| **Equality Act 2010** | In force | No discrimination; anticipatory duty to make reasonable adjustments (for example text alternatives to voice bots) |
| Public Sector Bodies Accessibility Regulations 2018 | In force | Accessible public-sector websites and apps (WCAG 2.2 AA under current guidance, verify) |
| **FCA rules (Consumer Duty, SM&CR, Principles)** | In force; FCA said on June 2, 2026 it will not add AI-specific rules | Good outcomes, vulnerable-customer support and access to human help for regulated firms |
| Ofcom General Conditions and persistent-misuse policy | In force | Valid caller ID; limits on silent and abandoned calls (background: about 3% abandoned calls per campaign per 24 hours, verify) |
| Sector cold-calling bans | In force (pensions, claims management; verify list) | Outbound AI calls may be prohibited for these products |
| Copyright, Designs and Patents Act 1988 | In force; government report of March 18, 2026 ruled out a broad training exception | Licences needed for training content unless an exception applies |
| NIS Regulations 2018; Cyber Security and Resilience Bill | In force / pending (introduced November 12, 2025) | Incident reporting and supplier security |
| Regulating for Growth Bill | Pending (announced May 13, 2026) | AI sandboxes; no standalone AI Act |
| CMA guidance on complying with consumer law when using AI agents | Guidance; first published March 9, 2026; updated October 30, 2026 with a joint DRCF statement (CMA, FCA, ICO and Ofcom) on agentic AI (T1) | Consumer-law expectations for agentic AI, including customer-service agents; the CMA can now fine up to 10% of global turnover |
| AI Cyber Security Code of Practice (January 31, 2025) | Guidance | 13 principles for secure AI; basis for ETSI TS 104 223 (Part 3.4) |

### Penalties: UK laws

| Law | Maximum penalties | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| UK GDPR and Data Protection Act 2018 | GBP 17.5 million or 4% of worldwide annual turnover; GBP 8.7 million or 2% for lesser infringements | Higher of the two |
| PECR (as amended by the DUAA) | GBP 17.5 million or 4% of worldwide annual turnover | Aligned with UK GDPR levels in 2026 |
| Online Safety Act 2023 | GBP 18 million or 10% of qualifying worldwide revenue, whichever is greater; business disruption orders; criminal liability for senior managers in some cases | Ofcom |
| Digital Markets, Competition and Consumers Act 2024 | Consumer-law fines up to 10% of global turnover; additional daily penalties (verify) | CMA direct enforcement |
| Equality Act 2010 | Uncapped compensation set by courts and tribunals | |
| FCA regime | Unlimited fines; redress schemes | Regulated firms |
| Communications Act (Ofcom persistent misuse and General Conditions) | Persistent misuse penalties up to GBP 2 million; General Conditions breaches up to 10% of relevant turnover (background; verify) | Silent and abandoned calls |
| NIS Regulations 2018 | Up to GBP 17 million | |

## 1.8 International treaty

| Instrument | Status | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| **Council of Europe Framework Convention on Artificial Intelligence and Human Rights, Democracy and the Rule of Law** | Opened for signature September 5, 2024; signed by the EU, UK and US among others (ratification status verify) | Principles on transparency, oversight, accountability and remedies for AI systems |

## 1.9 Proposed laws and rules likely in the next 12 to 18 months

This section covers proposals that would add new obligations for virtual agents between now (November 2026) and mid-2028, led by "right to a human" measures like California's AB 1609. It also lists obligations that are already enacted and dated within that window, because they are certain.

### 1.9.1 How to read the outlook ratings

The outlook is a judgment based on each proposal's stage, sponsorship, the legislative calendar and comparable laws. It is not a prediction of the outcome.

| Rating | Meaning |
|---|---|
| **Certain** | Enacted or adopted, and the source is T1 (primary text checked); only the effective date is in the future |
| **High** | Agency rule in final stages, government bill with priority, or a bill that has passed one chamber with broad support |
| **Medium** | Introduced with a credible path (committee action, carry-over, governor or agency support, or copies elsewhere) |
| **Low** | Introduced only, opposed, or facing a session deadline; likely to need reintroduction |

**Calendar facts that drive the ratings (verify dates):** the 119th US Congress ends January 3, 2027, so federal bills not passed by then die and must be reintroduced; California's and Pennsylvania's 2025 to 2026 sessions end November 30, 2026; Michigan's and New York's sessions end December 31, 2026; New Jersey's 2026 to 2027 session continues into 2028; Illinois's 104th General Assembly ends in January 2027; Texas's next regular session starts January 12, 2027 (bill prefiling from November 9, 2026); Virginia's 2027 session starts in January 2027.

### 1.9.3 Right-to-a-human and customer-service AI proposals

| Proposal | Jurisdiction | Stage (November 2026) | What it would require | Outlook |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **NYC Department of Consumer and Worker Protection rule on automated customer service ("doom loops")** | New York City | Proposed October 20, 2026. The notice of hearing (T1) gives November 19, 2026 for the hearing and comments; the DCWP rules page now lists December 18, 2026 (11:00 am) for both. Treat the window as open to December 18 until DCWP confirms (Rulecomments@dcwp.nyc.gov); comments were posted through November 22; effective date not set | Clear and conspicuous disclosure at the start of an automated interaction, in the language being used, that the consumer is dealing with an automated system; easy access to a human representative, including a one-touch option; applies to any channel where substantially all communication is automated; reported duty to keep a machine-readable log of every automated interaction for at least three years | **High** (agency rule; likely adopted with changes in 2027) |
| **Illinois HB 3653** | Illinois | Introduced (104th General Assembly); no reported movement | Amend the Consumer Fraud Act so businesses using automated phone or text systems offer a human-representative option at the start of the interaction during business hours | **Low to medium** (likely reintroduced in 2027 if it lapses) |
| **New Jersey S965 and A3663** | New Jersey | Introduced in the 2026 to 2027 session | Callers to state and local government entities may press zero to reach a live operator or leave a message during business hours (government only); a resolution (SR60) urges health insurers and pharmacy providers to do the same | **Medium** for government lines; private-sector mandate not yet proposed |
| **New York A8762 and A10796** | New York State | In committee | State-agency phone lines must offer a live-representative option before other menu prompts and may not rely exclusively on automated systems (government only) | **Low to medium** (session ends December 31, 2026; reintroduction likely) |
| **Keep Call Centers in America Act (S.2495; H.R.4954)** | US federal | Introduced July and August 2025; referred to committee | Disclose at the start that AI is in use; on request, transfer immediately to a human agent located in the United States; FTC enforcement with annual compliance certification; 120 days' notice to the Department of Labor before moving call-center work overseas | **Low** (dies January 3, 2027 unless passed; likely reintroduced) |
| **People-First Chatbot Act (H.R. 9619)** | US federal | Introduced July 9, 2026 (Reps. Foushee and Casar) | Based on model legislation from Fairplay, Consumer Federation of America and EPIC: regular disclosure that chatbots are not people; limits on training with minors' data and consent for adults' data; safety assessments; private right of action; trade press reports a disclosure and human-transfer duty for customer service (verify bill text) | **Low** (likely reintroduced) |
| **EU Digital Fairness Act** | EU | Commission proposal COM(2026) 1100 final, adopted November 11, 2026 (T1: Commission page and proposal text, procedure 2026/0356(COD)); not law. Would replace the Unfair Commercial Practices Directive (2005/29/EC) by regulation and amend the Consumer Rights Directive through a Simplification Directive | Article 25 (T1): after a contract, a consumer may request human communication when other means fail, free of charge, during the trader's business hours and in the same language (investment services excluded). Member states set penalties; the Commission fines through the revised CPC Regulation (the 6% headline is reported by Euronews, T2). The Commission's fitness check reported that 44% of consumers struggled to resolve issues because they could only use chatbots | **Proposal certain; adoption within 18 months unlikely** (EU legislation usually takes 18 to 30 months). Gartner predicts an EU right to talk to a human by 2028 |
| **UK "right to talk to a human"** | UK | Citizens Advice campaign (September 22, 2026) for energy, banking, telecoms and internet providers; Parliament petition 776511 for a working phone line to a human at every company; no government commitment found | Guaranteed human contact for essential services | **Low** for legislation within 18 months; **medium** for regulator guidance (Ofcom is reviewing AI in telecoms; see 1.9.4) |

### 1.9.5 Common obligations in the proposals, and what to build now

| Likely obligation | Proposals that contain it | Build or test now (corner-case catalog IDs) |
|---|---|---|
| Disclose AI at the start of every interaction, in the language and medium used | NYC rule, Keep Call Centers Act, People-First Chatbot Act, Senior Chatbot Protection Act, GUARD Act, Louisiana HB 686, FCC NPRM | Disclosure engine outside the model (M1, G1) |
| Human option on request, one-touch, on every channel | NYC rule, Illinois HB 3653, Keep Call Centers Act, EU DFA (expected), UK campaigns | Universal "agent" and "0" handling (F1, F2) |
| Time limits for reaching a human | California AB 1609 (15 minutes or callback) | Measure time-to-human (F6); callback scheduling (F3) |
| Human located in the United States | Keep Call Centers Act; People-First Chatbot Act (reported) | Routing able to select agents by location; record agent location per transfer |
| Interaction logs kept for years | NYC rule (three years, machine-readable, reported) | Retention policy for transcripts and decisions that also respects privacy deletion duties |
| Liability for inaccurate bot information | New York A222 (and the Air Canada principle) | Ground answers in authoritative policy (A1 to A8) |
| No impersonation of humans or licensed professionals | New York S7263 / S10675 / A6545, CHATBOT Act, state laws | G1, G2; scope limits for medical, legal and financial advice (A7) |
| Protections for minors and older adults | GUARD Act, Senior Chatbot Protection Act, Virginia, Ireland Bill 93, UK OSA amendment, state model bills | H1 to H3, H6; age assurance |
| Disclosure and consent for AI-made calls | Louisiana HB 686, FCC NPRM, Keep Call Centers Act | M1, M3 |
| Human intervention in automated financial decisions | EU CCD2 and Distance Financial Services Directive (now in force); California ADMT | M5 |

## 1.10 Worldwide AI laws and regulations (outside the US, EU and UK)

Most countries still regulate AI through existing privacy, consumer, online-safety and sector laws. A smaller group now has binding AI statutes (for example South Korea, Vietnam, Kazakhstan, China's measures and Russia's foundation-model law), and several have principle-based "basic acts" without penalties (Japan, Taiwan). Counts of "countries with AI laws" differ widely by definition; the IAPP tracker covers about two dozen jurisdictions in depth. Status and penalty entries below come largely from law-firm and news sources and should be confirmed locally. Sections 1.10.8 (Latin America and the Caribbean) and 1.10.9 (Asia-Pacific) give more detailed, more recent tables; where they differ from the summary tables, the detailed sections are more current.

### 1.10.1 Americas

| Country | Law or instrument | Status | Relevance to virtual agents | Maximum penalties |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Canada (federal)** | No AI act (AIDA died January 2025); national strategy "AI for All" (June 2026); Bill C-36 (Protecting Privacy and Consumer Data Act; first reading June 15, 2026; penalties up to CAD 10 million or 3% of global revenue; new Digital Safety and Data Protection Commission, T1) and Bill C-34 (Safe Social Media Act, covering platforms and AI chatbot services) | Bills at second reading (October 2026) | Automated-decision notices; chatbot safety duties if C-34 passes | PIPEDA fines up to CAD 100,000 (background); CASL administrative penalties up to CAD 10 million per violation for businesses (background; verify) |
| Canada (Quebec) | Law 25 (private-sector privacy) | In force | Notice of decisions based solely on automated processing; right to know factors and to submit observations; privacy impact assessments | Administrative penalties up to the greater of CAD 10 million or 2% of worldwide turnover; penal fines up to the greater of CAD 25 million or 4% (doubled for repeat offenses) |
| Canada (provinces) | Ontario Bill 194 (public-sector AI, 2024); Manitoba Public Sector AI and Cybersecurity Governance Act (June 2026); Ontario AI disclosure in job postings (reported effective January 1, 2026; verify) | In force | Public-sector agents; recruiting agents | Varies |
| **Brazil** | LGPD (data protection); AI bill PL 2338/2023 | LGPD in force; PL 2338 passed the Senate (December 2024) and awaits the Chamber special committee; 2026 elections make a 2027 vote likely | Review of automated decisions (LGPD); risk-based AI duties and rights to explanation and human review (bill) | LGPD: 2% of Brazilian revenue, up to BRL 50 million per infraction; PL 2338 proposes the same ceiling |
| **Mexico** | No federal AI law (the constitutional reform needed to allow one is in committee); new Federal Law on Protection of Personal Data Held by Private Parties (LFPDPPP, March 21, 2025; INAI dissolved, data-protection authority now the anti-corruption and good-governance ministry, SABG); May 14, 2026 reform of copyright and labor laws requiring consent for AI use of performers' voice and image | Data law and May 2026 reform in force; AI bills pending | Data-protection duties; consent for AI voice and likeness of performers (see Section 1.10.8) | LFPDPPP fines (ranges verify) |
| **Peru** | Law 31814 (2023) and implementing regulation | In force | Risk-based AI obligations; the only enacted AI statute in Latin America per one tracker | Verify |
| **Chile** | AI bill (Boletín 16821-19, second stage in the Senate); Personal Data Protection Law 21.719 | Law 21.719 legally starts December 1, 2026, but a bill moving the start to October 15, 2027 has passed the Senate and a Chamber committee (verify final vote) | Risk-based AI duties (bill); data-protection duties including automated decisions | Data law: fines reported up to about 4% of annual revenue for large companies (verify) |
| **Colombia** | CONPES 4144 (2025) AI policy; ethical framework (2021); bills | Policy | Guidance | Data protection law penalties |
| **Argentina** | Recommendations for reliable AI (2023); data protection and consumer laws | Guidance | Existing law applies | Existing-law penalties |

| Country | Law or instrument | Status | Relevance to virtual agents | Maximum penalties |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **China** | Interim Measures for Generative AI Services (2023); deep synthesis and algorithm recommendation rules; AI content labeling measures and standard GB 45438-2025 (September 1, 2025); amended Cybersecurity Law with AI provisions (January 1, 2026); **Interim Measures for Anthropomorphic AI Interaction Services** (adopted April 10, 2026, in force July 15, 2026) | In force | Registration and security assessment for public generative AI; explicit and implicit labels on AI content; for human-like companion services: disclosure that users are talking to AI, human takeover when users raise suicide or self-harm, tiered supervision and protections for minors | Cybersecurity Law: general cap reported at RMB 10 million for critical operators and RMB 1 million for others, individuals up to RMB 1 million (sources vary); PIPL: up to RMB 50 million or 5% of prior-year turnover for serious violations (background; verify); suspension of services |
| **South Korea** | AI Basic Act and Enforcement Decree | In force January 22, 2026; fines deferred for at least one year except serious harm | High-impact AI duties (risk management, human oversight, explanation); notice and labeling for generative AI; domestic representative for foreign providers; MSIT can still investigate and order corrections | Administrative fines up to KRW 30 million |
| **Japan** | AI Promotion Act (Act No. 53 of 2025); Basic AI Plan (first adopted December 23, 2025; revised July 14, 2026); 2026 APPI amendment (passed July 10, 2026) | In force (principle-based) | Effort-based duties to cooperate with government; existing privacy and consumer law applies | No AI-specific penalties; the 2026 APPI amendment introduces Japan's first administrative surcharge for privacy violations (see Section 1.10.9) |
| **Taiwan** | AI Basic Act | In force January 14, 2026 | Principles; sector regulators to issue detailed rules based on a risk framework from the digital ministry | No penalties yet |
| **Vietnam** | Law on Artificial Intelligence (No. 134/2025/QH15); Decree 142/2026/ND-CP (May 1, 2026); high-risk AI list (effective August 15, 2026) | In force March 1, 2026; 18-month transition for legacy systems in health, education and finance | Three-tier risk classification; machine-readable labels on AI audio, images and video; local point of contact for foreign high-risk providers | Administrative penalties, criminal liability for serious cases and civil compensation; revenue-based penalties removed from the final law |
| **India** | IT Rules amendment on synthetically generated information (notified February 10, 2026, in force February 20, 2026); Digital Personal Data Protection Act 2023 and Rules 2025 (phased, full obligations about May 2027); non-binding AI governance guidelines (background) | In force / phasing in | Prominent labels and embedded provenance metadata for AI content; verification duties for large platforms; consent, notice and children's data rules | DPDP: up to INR 250 crore for security-safeguard failures, up to INR 200 crore for breach-notification failures, INR 150 to 200 crore for children's-data violations (sources vary); IT Rules: loss of safe harbor in some cases |
| **Australia** | No AI act; National AI Plan (December 2, 2025; mandatory guardrails dropped); Privacy Act automated-decision transparency (from December 10, 2026); eSafety Age-Restricted Material Codes covering AI companion chatbots; social media minimum age (December 10, 2025); AI Safety Institute (2026) | In force / phasing in | Companion chatbots able to produce sexual, violent or self-harm material must confirm users are 18 or older; privacy-policy disclosure of automated decisions | Privacy Act serious interference: greater of AUD 50 million, three times the benefit, or 30% of adjusted turnover; Online Safety Act penalties (background: up to about AUD 49.5 million; verify) |

| **Singapore** | No binding AI law; Model AI Governance Framework for Agentic AI (January 22, 2026); PDPA | Guidance plus PDPA | Agent-governance expectations; data protection | PDPA: up to 10% of annual Singapore turnover (for turnover above SGD 10 million) or SGD 1 million (verify which applies) |
| **Malaysia** | Online Safety Act 2025 (in force January 1, 2026); AI Governance Bill | AI bill to be tabled (2026) | Online-safety duties; future AI governance | PDPA penalties (verify) |
| **Thailand** | Draft Act on Artificial Intelligence (new version July 2, 2026) | Pending | Risk-based duties, extraterritorial reach | PDPA penalties (background: up to THB 5 million; verify) |
| **Indonesia** | Presidential regulation on AI (expected); Personal Data Protection Law | AI rule pending | Data protection | PDP Law administrative fines (background: up to 2% of annual revenue; verify) |
| **Philippines** | Domestic AI bill; proposal for an ASEAN AI framework as 2026 chair; Data Privacy Act | Pending | | Data Privacy Act penalties |
| **Hong Kong** | Voluntary AI guidance; Personal Data (Privacy) Ordinance | Guidance | | PDPO penalties |
| **Kazakhstan** | Law on Artificial Intelligence (No. 230-VIII) | Signed November 17, 2025; in force January 18, 2026 | Risk-based classification; high-risk systems treated as critical digital infrastructure | Verify |

### 1.10.7 Global patterns for virtual agents

1. **AI disclosure and labeling** are near-universal: China, South Korea, Vietnam, India and the EU require labels or notices for AI interactions or content.
2. **Companion-style and human-like chatbots** face specific rules in China (human takeover on self-harm cues), Australia (age confirmation) and several US states.
3. **Data-protection laws carry the largest penalties** in most countries (turnover-based fines in China, Brazil, Quebec, Australia, India's fixed high caps).
4. **Local representation** is required for foreign providers in South Korea and Vietnam.
5. **Principle-based "basic acts"** (Japan, Taiwan) set direction without penalties; sector regulators supply the detail.

### 1.10.8 Latin America and the Caribbean in detail

Scope: AI laws and bills, data-protection rules on automated decisions, customer-service (SAC) rules, telemarketing and robocall rules, deepfake, likeness and election rules, and biometrics. Currency conversions are approximate. Court cases and regulator decisions are in Section 2.14.

Inform people that processing is automated; right to object; explanation of logic where decisions have direct impact; human review of automated decisions with legal or significant effects (the amendment removed the express clause and now points to LOPDP rights generally). Risk management before development; impact assessment "where applicable" after the amendment. Processors (e.g., software vendors) are bound only where they have access, visibility or effective control over the data; vendors must tell the controller how the system works, its limits, foreseeable risks and controls; a vendor claiming no access must keep evidence. | LOPDP regime (up to 1% of business volume). | [LEXIS, 10 Mar 2026](https://www.lexis.com.ec/noticias/registro-oficial-del-dia-superintendencia-de-proteccion-de-datos-expide-norma-para-el-uso-de-inteligencia-artificial); [NMS Law, 14 Sep 2026](https://nmslaw.com.ec/blog/2026/09/14/ecuador-spdp-reforma-norma-inteligencia-artificial-obligaciones/) |

### 1.10.9 Asia-Pacific in detail

Scope: China, Japan, South Korea, India, Australia, New Zealand, Singapore, Hong Kong, Taiwan, Vietnam, Indonesia, Malaysia, Philippines, Thailand, Pakistan, Bangladesh, Sri Lanka and ASEAN. Laws already in Section 1.10.2 are not repeated except as updates. Court cases and regulator decisions are in Section 2.15.

#### Laws

| Jurisdiction | Law or instrument | Status | Requirements relevant to virtual agents | Maximum penalties | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| China | National standard GB/T 47746-2026, "Customer Contact Service: Synergy Requirements between Artificial and Intelligent Customer Service" (number confirmed in Chinese media, T2) | In force Sep 1, 2026 (voluntary GB/T standard) | AI handles routine queries; humans take complex and safety matters. Easy-to-find route to a human (keywords such as "转人工", menu, keypad, voice, auto-transfer). Pass context so customer does not repeat. Label AI-generated replies and add risk warnings. Price, refund, compensation or contract changes need human or standardized confirmation. Controls against AI "false promises". | None directly (voluntary). Likely used as a benchmark in consumer disputes and regulator reviews (verify). | https://www.kbc.co.ke/china-implements-new-national-standard-for-ai-customer-service/ ; https://www.lexology.com/library/detail.aspx?g=1f7c4b99-c8ec-4164-bbf7-093bb66eb82f |
| China | Security Management Measures for the Application of Facial Recognition Technology (CAC and Ministry of Public Security) | In force Jun 1, 2025 | Face recognition cannot be the only verification option where alternatives exist; offer reasonable alternatives. Impact assessment before use. Filing with provincial CAC when facial data of more than 100,000 people is held (verify). Excludes R&D and algorithm training. Relevant to face or video ID&V in apps and kiosks. | PIPL penalties apply (up to RMB 50M or 5% of prior-year revenue) (verify linkage). | https://www.hunton.com/privacy-and-cybersecurity-law-blog/china-releases-new-rules-regarding-the-use-of-facial-recognition-technology ; https://cset.georgetown.edu/publication/china-facial-recognition-security-measures/ |
| Japan | Amendments to the Act on the Protection of Personal Information (APPI), 2026 | Enacted. Passed Jul 10, 2026; promulgated Jul 17, 2026. Commencement needs a cabinet order (not yet made); stronger criminal penalties from Jan 17, 2027 are reported (T2 only); surcharge and main reforms by Jul 17, 2028 at latest (verify dates) | New administrative surcharge. New rules for children's data and for certain biometric data. New exception for "statistical compilation, etc." that eases use of data for AI development, with no reuse beyond that purpose. Applies to foreign firms serving people in Japan. | Surcharge based on financial benefit from the violation. Only when five conditions are met, including consideration received, lack of care, serious harm and more than 1,000 people affected (verify formula). | https://www.pwc.com/jp/en/knowledge/news/legal-news/legal-20260827-6.html ; https://abe-legal.jp/en/news/appi-amendment-2026-ai-data |

Refusal right does not apply where the decision rests on consent, contract or law, but explanation still does. Controller must publish criteria and procedures. Credit scoring follows the Credit Information Act instead. | PIPA penalty surcharges up to 3% of total revenue for some violations (verify); administrative fines for other breaches (verify amount for Art. 37-2). | https://resourcehub.bakermckenzie.com/en/resources/global-data-and-cyber-handbook/asia-pacific/south-korea/topics/artificial-intelligence-profiling-and-automated-decision-making ; https://prighter.com/resources/laws/korean-pipa/main/articles/article-37-2 |

## 1.12 Emotion and sentiment inference in contact centers

Contact centers often score sentiment or emotion from voice tone, facial video or text, for customers and for agents. The rules differ by who is scored and where.

| Instrument | Status | What it means for a virtual-agent program | Possible penalties |
|---|---|---|---|
| EU AI Act Article 5(1)(f), prohibition on emotion recognition in the workplace and education | In force since February 2, 2025 | Prohibits AI systems that infer emotions of people in the workplace and in education, except for medical or safety reasons. Scoring agents' emotions from voice or facial analysis falls inside the ban in EU workplaces. Scoring customers is a different question (verify the use case) | Up to EUR 35 million or 7% of worldwide annual turnover (prohibited practices) |
| EU AI Act, Annex III employment and worker-management systems | Stand-alone high-risk obligations apply December 2, 2027 under the Digital Omnibus, Regulation (EU) 2026/1744 (verify) | Tools that evaluate or monitor workers are high-risk. Agent-performance scoring that uses sentiment is a likely case (verify the Annex III point) | Up to EUR 15 million or 3% (other obligations) |
| GDPR Articles 9 and 22 | In force | Emotion or health inferences can be special-category data. Decisions based solely on automated processing need a lawful basis, information and, where applicable, human review | Up to EUR 20 million or 4% |

No catalog case covers agent sentiment scoring yet. Catalog case Q10 was added for it.

# Part 2. Court cases and enforcement decisions involving AI

Cases are grouped by the issue AI raised. Each entry gives the forum, date, what happened, the reported outcome or status, and why it matters for a virtual agent. Allegations in pending cases are unproven. No large jury verdict awarding damages against a virtual-agent operator was found; most monetary outcomes are tribunal awards, settlements, regulator penalties and sanctions.

## 2.1 What a bot says: misstatements, liability and defamation

| Case | Forum and date | What happened | Outcome or status | Lesson for virtual agents |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Moffatt v. Air Canada** | British Columbia Civil Resolution Tribunal, February 2024 | Chatbot told a passenger he could claim a bereavement fare after travel, contrary to policy | Airline liable for negligent misrepresentation; the "separate legal entity" argument rejected; award of about CAD 650.88 plus interest and fees (about CAD 812 total) | The company owns what its bot says; ground answers in authoritative policy |
| **Clinic chatbot decision** | Higher Regional Court of Hamm, Germany, May 2026 (reported) | A clinic's website chatbot gave invented medical specialist titles | Companies can be liable for misleading chatbot statements; appeal to the Federal Court of Justice reportedly allowed | Professional claims need verified data and hard limits |
| **Publishers v. Google (AI Overviews)** | Munich Regional Court, Germany, May or June 2026 (dates differ) | AI summaries repeated false claims about two publishers | Google treated as author of the summaries; injunction granted | Generated statements about named businesses can be unlawful |
| **Walters v. OpenAI** | Superior Court of Gwinnett County, Georgia, May 2025 | ChatGPT produced a false summary accusing a radio host of embezzlement | Summary judgment for OpenAI (no proof of harm or fault; warnings and accuracy efforts considered) | Disclaimers and accuracy controls matter, but do not rely on them alone |
| **Wolf River Electric (LTL LED) v. Google** | Minnesota (federal, then reportedly remanded to state court January 2026) | AI Overview falsely said a solar company faced an attorney-general lawsuit; damages claimed of USD 110 to 210 million | Pending | False statements about businesses can cause measurable losses |
| **Starbuck v. Meta** | Delaware Superior Court, 2025 | AI chatbot made false statements about an activist | Settled August 2025 (terms undisclosed; Starbuck reportedly hired as an adviser) | Early settlement pressure in AI defamation |
| **Starbuck v. Google** | Delaware Superior Court, filed October 2025 | Similar allegations | Pending (motion to dismiss filed) | |
| **Chevrolet dealer and DPD incidents** | Not litigated (2023, 2024) | Bot "agreed" to a USD 1 car; delivery bot swore at a customer | No damages; reputational harm; bots withdrawn | Commitment and tone controls |

## 2.2 Safety, minors and wrongful death

| Case | Forum and date | What happened | Outcome or status | Lesson |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Garcia v. Character Technologies and Google** | US District Court, Middle District of Florida, No. 6:24-cv-01903, filed 2024 | Mother alleged a companion chatbot contributed to her son's suicide | Court declined to dismiss on First Amendment grounds (2025). Settlement in principle announced January 7, 2026 for this and related family suits; the court dismissed without prejudice that day with 90 days to finalize. A reported August 4, 2026 extension is not confirmed (Section 2.16). Final docket status to verify on PACER | Minors, companion personas and long emotional sessions need hard safety controls |
| **Raine v. OpenAI** and related suits | California state and federal courts, 2025 to 2026 | Families allege chatbot conversations contributed to suicides | Pending; a federal court reportedly ruled OpenAI must defend a related wrongful-death suit | Long-conversation safety degradation is a litigation risk |
| **Other Character.AI claims** | Including a separate Colorado wrongful-death case | Similar allegations | Pending (verify) | |

## 2.3 Privacy, recording, biometrics and automated decisions

| Case or decision | Forum and date | What happened | Outcome or status | Lesson |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Ambriz v. Google** | US District Court, Northern District of California, February 10, 2025 | Callers alleged Google's Contact Center AI listened to and analyzed customer-service calls | Motion to dismiss denied: Google's capability to use call data for its own purposes was enough to state a CIPA wiretap claim. Oct 22, 2026 (T2): arbitration compelled for Verizon, Uber and Discover callers; Home Depot callers' claims dismissed with leave to amend; now captioned In re Google Cloud Contact Center AI Privacy Litigation | AI call-analytics vendors can be third-party "eavesdroppers"; get consent before recording or analysis |
| **Popa v. Microsoft** | US Court of Appeals, Ninth Circuit, August 26, 2025 (153 F.4th 784) | Website session-replay case under Pennsylvania's wiretap law (not CIPA) | Affirmed dismissal for lack of Article III standing (T2); commentators see it weakening the "capability" theory | Standing defenses are developing, but consent remains the safe design |
| **In re Otter.ai Privacy Litigation** | US District Court, Northern District of California (No. 5:25-cv-06911), consolidated 2025 | AI notetaker joined meetings and recorded participants who had not consented | Core wiretap, CIPA and Illinois biometric claims reported to survive; other claims dismissed; further rulings pending (sources conflict on timing) | Meeting and call assistants need all-party notice and consent |
| **Dialpad CIPA case** | San Francisco Superior Court | AI features in a phone platform | Dismissed: telephone-provider immunity | Provider status can matter |
| **Gutierrez v. Converse Inc.** | US Court of Appeals, Ninth Circuit, No. 24-4797 (July 9, 2025; unpublished memorandum) | Chat-widget messages processed by Salesforce | Summary judgment for Converse affirmed: no evidence the vendor read or tried to read the chats, and capability alone is not enough; a concurrence says the first clause of CIPA section 631(a) does not cover internet communications | Chat and voice are treated differently under CIPA; vendor access and use of content is the key fact |
| **Lisota v. Heartland Dental** | US District Court, Northern District of Illinois, January 13, 2026 | AI voice and call-analytics claims | Reported win for the vendor and deployer | Outcomes vary by statute and facts |
| **Texas v. Meta (CUBI)** | Texas state court, settled July 2024 | Facial-recognition data collected without consent | USD 1.4 billion settlement | Biometric consent failures are very expensive |
| **Texas v. Google** | Texas state court, settled May 2025 | Data collection claims including biometrics | About USD 1.4 billion settlement | Same |
| **Clearview AI BIPA class action** | US District Court, Northern District of Illinois, 2024 to 2025 | Scraped face images | Settlement giving the class a share of the company (about 23%); approval details verify | |
| **Information Commissioner v. Clearview AI** | UK Upper Tribunal, [2025] UKUT 319 (AAC), October 6, 2025 | GBP 7.5 million ICO fine for face scraping | ICO's jurisdiction upheld and case remitted; Clearview given permission to appeal to the Court of Appeal (December 19, 2025); outcome pending | Foreign AI providers can fall under UK GDPR through behavioral monitoring |
| **Dutch Data Protection Authority v. Clearview AI** | Netherlands, 2024 | Face scraping | EUR 30.5 million fine (verify) | |
| **OQ v. Land Hessen (SCHUFA), C-634/21** | Court of Justice of the EU, December 7, 2023 | Credit score used by lenders | A score that plays a decisive role in a third party's decision can itself be an automated decision under GDPR Article 22 (background; verify) | Scoring and triage by AI can trigger Article 22 duties |
| **CK v. Dun & Bradstreet Austria, C-203/22** | Court of Justice of the EU, February 27, 2025 | Phone contract refused after automated credit check | Data subjects are entitled to a clear explanation of how the decision was reached (not necessarily the full algorithm); trade secrets handled through court review | Be able to explain each automated decision in plain terms |
| **Italian Garante v. Luka Inc. (Replika)** | Italy, April 10, 2025 | Companion chatbot: lawful basis, age verification, transparency | EUR 5 million fine | Companion bots need age assurance and a lawful basis |
| **Italian Garante v. OpenAI** | Italy, 2024; Rome court, March 18, 2026 | EUR 15 million fine for ChatGPT data processing | Reported annulled on procedural (one-stop-shop) grounds (T2); a Garante page snippet says the court accepted the opposition on the merits, so the outcome is unresolved; Irish DPC scrutiny continues | Annulment is not clearance |
| **Italian Garante v. Character Technologies (Character.AI)** | Italy, decision July 3, 2026 (release July 9) | Companion chatbot: GDPR Articles 5(2), 12 to 14, 24, 25(2), 27 and 35 | EUR 158,000 fine; orders on age verification, cooling-off periods and private-by-default profiles for minors; 120 days to report (T1, Garante decisions 10269571 and 10269594; appealable) | Age assurance and default privacy for minors are enforced in the EU |
| **Dutch Data Protection Authority v. Uber** | Netherlands, decision August 17, 2026 (public August 21) | Automated suspension of driver accounts (fraud flags, ratings) without human review, 2020 to 2022 | Reported fine of EUR 824.99 million; Uber contesting (T2; the Authority's own text not retrieved) | Automated account suspensions need meaningful human review |
| **Irish DPC inquiry into X Internet Unlimited Company (Grok)** | Ireland, opened February 17, 2026 | Generation of sexualised images of identifiable people, including children; GDPR Articles 5, 6, 25 and 35 | Pending (T1 press release) | Generative-AI image outputs can trigger inquiries |
| **Uber and Ola drivers (automated decisions)** | Amsterdam District Court and Court of Appeal, 2021 and 2023 | Drivers challenged automated deactivations | Courts ordered transparency and found some decisions solely automated (background; verify) | Automated account suspensions need human review |

## 2.10 Patterns across the case law

1. **Companies are responsible for their bots' statements** (Air Canada, Hamm, Munich).
2. **Recording and analysis without consent is the most common privacy theory** against AI customer-service tools (Ambriz, Otter.ai), with damages per violation.
3. **Biometric consent failures produce the largest settlements** (Texas v. Meta and Google, Clearview).
4. **Automated decisions must be explainable and reviewable by a person** (Dun & Bradstreet, SCHUFA, CMS guidance in the health cases).
5. **Vendor exposure is split.** Courts have let claims against vendors proceed where the vendor can use the data (Taylor, Gladstone, Ambriz). Other courts have dismissed similar claims on third-party, standing, consent or agency grounds (see Sections 2.3 and 2.12.3). Do not assume that a vendor shields the business, or that a vendor creates exposure, without checking the contract and the forum.
6. **Marketing claims about AI are enforced** by the FTC, SEC and state attorneys general.
7. **Harm to minors and vulnerable users drives the highest-severity litigation** (Character.AI, OpenAI suits).
8. **Fabricated content is sanctionable** wherever AI output is used in formal proceedings.

## 2.11 Reported consumer harms from AI and automated decisions (2024 to 2026)

These are investigations, regulator records, news reports and surveys rather than court rulings. They show where automated decisions have hurt people and where regulators and litigators are likely to look next. Figures from vendor-sponsored surveys and secondary outlets are flagged; check the original report before citing any number.

| Area | Report and date | What was reported | Response or status | Lesson for virtual agents (catalog IDs) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Health coverage: Medicare's WISeR AI prior-authorization pilot** | CMS records obtained under FOIA (Fierce Healthcare), spring 2026; Healthcare Dive, April 24, 2026 | Thousands of requests exceeded the pilot's three-day turnaround goal and several hundred were still unanswered at the end of March 2026 (one 83 days old); system downtime; provider complaints about a vendor in Ohio; approval times of four to eight weeks reported in Washington; one contractor reportedly denied 53% of the requests it decided in Washington in the first quarter (secondhand figure, verify) | Senate Democrats filed a resolution to repeal the pilot (May 2026; outcome not found); the Electronic Frontier Foundation sued CMS for information on the algorithm; pilot runs in six states through 2031 | Automated decisions need human review, deadline tracking and escalation (F7, M5, N2) |
| **Health coverage: Medicare Advantage denials** | Senate Permanent Subcommittee on Investigations staff report, October 2024; follow-up letter to UnitedHealthcare, October 9, 2025 | Prior-authorization denial rates rose as automated processes were adopted (UnitedHealthcare); insurers disputed the findings | States are requiring licensed clinicians to make AI-assisted denials (for example Washington SB 5395, 2026); NAIC piloting an AI Systems Evaluation Tool in about 12 states (2026) | Human decision-makers for adverse outcomes; record the AI's role |
| **Retail facial recognition (UK)** | Guardian, Retail Gazette and BBC reporting, February to August 2026 | Shoppers wrongly ejected or banned after Facewatch alerts at Sainsbury's (two cases in 2026, including Dulwich in August), Home Bargains and B&M; Facewatch reported almost 300,000 alerts to retailers in the first half of 2026; people struggle to clear their names | Sainsbury's paused cameras at the Dulwich store and blamed human error; Facewatch said its technology was not at fault; Big Brother Watch called for an end to the system | An AI alert is not a decision; staff need verification steps and a fast redress route (B-category identity checks, F7, N6) |
| **Welfare fraud scoring (UK)** | Guardian freedom-of-information investigation, 2024 | A DWP housing-benefit tool wrongly flagged more than 200,000 people for investigation; about two-thirds of "high risk" flags in three years were legitimate claims. The tool is rules-based, not machine learning. Separate reporting describes council risk scoring of about 540,000 applicants (secondary source) | Ongoing criticism; allegations of discrimination against single mothers and disabled people (unproven) | Measure false-positive rates and their effect on real people, not just detection rates |
| **Welfare fraud scoring (France)** | Coalition litigation before the Conseil d'État (filed October 2024; 25 organisations by January 2026) | The CNAF family-benefits risk score reportedly raises suspicion for low income, unemployment, minimum-income and disability benefits; a 2025 internal study reportedly recognized discriminatory effects | Défenseur des Droits found serious discrimination (opinion, October 2025); CNAF published the current algorithm's source code on January 15, 2026; case pending | Social-scoring-like risk models face AI Act prohibitions and discrimination claims |
| **Tenant screening** | *Louis v. SafeRent Solutions* (D. Mass.) | Scores allegedly denied housing to Black and Hispanic applicants using vouchers; one plaintiff had 16 years of positive rental history | Settlement of about USD 2.3 million (approved 2024, date verify); SafeRent stopped scoring voucher holders for five years; DOJ and HUD filed a statement of interest on the Fair Housing Act | Automated eligibility tools need disparate-impact testing (M5) |
| **Surveillance and personalized pricing** | Consumer Reports investigation of Instacart's AI pricing experiments (2025); House Oversight inquiry to Uber, Lyft, Expedia, Booking.com and Instacart (March 2026); Senate Judiciary subcommittee hearing (August 4, 2026) | Prices that vary by person based on personal data | FTC investigation of Instacart (late 2025); FTC proposed enforcement policy statement on personalized pricing (August 19, 2026; comments closed September 25); California Attorney General investigative sweep (2026); Maryland ban for large grocers and delivery apps from October 1, 2026; New Jersey grocery ban from August 1, 2027; Connecticut law reported to cover all retailers (verify); class actions against JetBlue (April and May 2026) | Agents that quote prices must use the posted or system-of-record price and disclose any personalization (A8) |
| **Account freezes and closures** | CFPB finding that fraud false positives often froze accounts for weeks (2024); 2026 news reports of rising freezes; AlgorithmWatch "False Positives" reporting (Europe) | Automated fraud and anti-money-laundering systems froze cards, blocked transfers and closed accounts, often without explanation | No single 2026 regulator report found | Agents handling locked accounts need clear explanations, escalation and status tracking (A6, F7, H3) |
| **Chatbot financial advice** | NerdWallet survey (September 2026); Pearl.com survey (2025) | Nearly a third of Americans who followed AI chatbot financial advice said it hurt their finances; about one in four Americans has asked a chatbot a financial question; 19% said they lost more than USD 100 following chatbot advice (Pearl.com) | Survey findings, not adjudicated harm | Scope limits on financial advice; disclaimers; human escalation (A7, A8) |
| **AI customer service** | Qualtrics 2026 CX Trends; Glance 2026; AnswerConnect (May 2026; 6,000 consumers in the US, UK and Canada); Citizens Advice (UK, September 2026); EU Digital Fairness fitness check | About one in five consumers who used AI for service saw no benefit, with reports of loops, deflection and denied refunds (Qualtrics); nearly nine in ten said losing human support reduced loyalty (Glance); frustration with AI agents rose from 54% to 59% (AnswerConnect); 14% of people who struggled to reach a human gave up (Citizens Advice); 44% of EU consumers struggled to resolve issues with chatbot-only service | Drives the right-to-a-human proposals in Section 1.9; most surveys are vendor-sponsored | Measure outcomes, not containment; guaranteed human path (F1 to F8, N1) |
| **General-purpose chatbots** | Axios, October 15, 2026 | FTC civil subpoenas to OpenAI and Anthropic in a consumer-protection probe covering security incidents, safeguarding practices and privacy policies | Investigation; no findings | Vendor due diligence on model providers' safety and incident practices |

## 2.12 Additional US court cases (November 2026 deep search)

These cases add to Sections 2.1 to 2.9. Several of the most relevant to contact-center virtual agents are wiretap (CIPA) suits against AI contact-center vendors and 2026 TCPA rulings on consent and AI voice. Sources are cited row by row.

### 2.12.1 Bot statements, liability and defamation

No US court ruling was found that holds a business to a promise made by its customer-service chatbot (that is, no US counterpart to Canada's *Moffatt v. Air Canada*). No ruling was found on whether Section 230 protects generative-AI output.

| Case | Court and date (with docket number if available) | Facts | Outcome or status | Lesson for virtual agents | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| *Battle v. Microsoft* | D. Md., No. 1:23-cv-01822 (tracker lists -01836, verify); order Oct 23, 2024 | Bing and Copilot answers confused the plaintiff with a convicted man of a similar name. | Court sent the case to arbitration under the Microsoft Services Agreement and stayed it. No merits ruling. | An arbitration clause in the user terms can move hallucination-defamation claims out of court. | [Order](https://storage.courtlistener.com/recap/gov.uscourts.mdd.540279/gov.uscourts.mdd.540279.48.0.pdf); [Akin Gump](https://www.akingump.com/en/insights/ai-law-and-regulation-tracker/microsoft-faces-defamation-lawsuit-in-maryland) |
| *Nippon Life Ins. Co. of America v. OpenAI Foundation* | N.D. Ill., No. 1:26-cv-02448; filed Mar 4, 2026 | ChatGPT told a disability claimant her settlement could be undone. It then helped her draft 44 or more filings, one citing a fake case. Her insurer sued the chatbot vendor. | Claims: tortious interference, abuse of process, unauthorized practice of law; $300K plus $10M punitive sought. OpenAI moved to dismiss May 15, 2026; motion pending. | Third parties harmed by bot advice may sue the vendor. Tailored legal or professional conclusions invite unauthorized-practice claims, and disclaimers alone may not protect. | [Epstein Becker Green](https://www.ebglaw.com/commercial-litigation-update/the-case-was-settled-but-chatgpt-thought-otherwise-a-dispute-poised-to-define-ai-legal-liability); [AI Lawsuit Tracker](https://ailawsuittracker.com/cases/nippon-life-insurance-company-of-america-v-openai-foundation-1-26-cv-02448/) |
| *xAI v. Bonta*; *X.AI LLC v. Ellison* (First Amendment challenges to state AI laws) | C.D. Cal., No. 2:25-cv-12295 (9th Cir. No. 26-1591); D. Minn., No. 0:26-cv-03425 (8th Cir. No. 26-2806) | xAI says California AB 2013 (training-data disclosure) is compelled speech. It also challenges Minnesota's ban on AI "nudification." | *Bonta*: preliminary injunction denied Mar 4, 2026; 9th Cir. argument set Nov 18, 2026. *Ellison*: injunction denied Sept 4, 2026; the 8th Cir. granted an injunction pending appeal Oct 2, 2026 (verify). | Courts have not settled whether AI output and AI disclosure rules are protected speech. Plan for state transparency laws to stay in force. | [AI Lawsuit Tracker: Bonta](https://ailawsuittracker.com/cases/xai-corp-v-rob-bonta-in-his-official-capacity-as-attorney-general-of-california/); [AI Lawsuit Tracker: Ellison](https://ailawsuittracker.com/cases/x-ai-llc-v-ellison-0-26-cv-03425/) |

No jury verdict and no federal MDL had been reported by Nov 14, 2026. The OpenAI cases are coordinated in a California state proceeding (JCCP).

The ruling may decide whether a chatbot is a "product" and whether its output is speech. | [AI Lawsuit Tracker](https://ailawsuittracker.com/cases/gavalas-v-google-llc-gemini-wrongful-death-action/) |

Claims under the Kentucky Consumer Protection Act and the Kentucky Consumer Data Protection Act; alleges bots posed as mental-health professionals to minors. | Active. Unclear whether the company's bankruptcy stay applies (verify). | Persona bots that reach minors carry both deceptive-practice and privacy exposure. | [WKMS](https://www.wkms.org/government-politics/2026-01-09/kentucky-ags-lawsuit-says-ai-company-preys-on-youth); [AI Lawsuit Tracker](https://ailawsuittracker.com/cases/kentucky-v-character-technologies-26-ci-00029/) |
| *Pennsylvania Dept. of State v. Character Technologies* | Pa. Commonwealth Court, No. 220 MD 2026; filed May 1, 2026 | A bot claimed to be a Pennsylvania-licensed psychiatrist and gave a fake license number. | Unlicensed practice of medicine under the Medical Practice Act; preliminary injunction sought. Active. | Never let a bot claim professional credentials. Licensing boards are now enforcers. | [Pa. Governor's Office](https://www.pa.gov/governor/newsroom/2026-press-releases/shapiro-administration-sues-character-ai-over-fake-medical-claim) |
| Eight-state AG suits v. OpenAI (AZ, AR, ID, IA, KS, MT, NE, WV) | Separate state courts; all filed Oct 14, 2026 (for example, Neb. No. D09CI260000488; W. Va. No. CC-26-2026-C-70) | Allege ChatGPT was marketed as safe for teens while being "overly agreeable." Also allege collection of minors' data and unsafe testing of AI agents. Florida filed separately June 1, 2026 (verify). | Newly filed. Seek penalties, age verification and data deletion. | Safety claims in marketing are themselves actionable. AGs want product-design injunctions. | [WDTV](https://www.wdtv.com/2026/10/14/west-virginia-attorney-general-joins-multi-state-lawsuit-against-openai/); [Open Class Actions](https://openclassactions.com/news/eight-states-sue-openai-chatgpt-safety-lawsuits-2026.php) |
| *Texas v. OpenAI and Sam Altman* | Texas state court (county not confirmed); filed Oct 23, 2026 (T1 state release) | Texas consumer-protection suit under the DTPA (the state release cites the DTPA only; a SCOPE Act count is not confirmed). It cites help given to mass shooters, encouragement of suicide, and teen addiction. | Seeks to block minors and to require approval of new models by a court-appointed evaluator. Active. | Remedies sought include review of models before release, not only money. | [Texas Tribune](https://www.texastribune.org/2026/10/23/ken-paxton-chatgpt-openai-lawsuit-texas-attorney-general/) |

### 2.12.3 Privacy, wiretap, recording and biometrics

| Case | Court and date (with docket number if available) | Facts | Outcome or status | Lesson for virtual agents | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| *Gladstone v. Amazon Web Services* | W.D. Wash., No. 2:23-cv-00491-TL; order July 2, 2024 | Capital One calls were recorded, transcribed and analyzed by Amazon Connect and Contact Lens. AWS terms let it use "ML Content" to improve its own services. Callers got no notice. | Motion to dismiss denied under CIPA § 632, applying the "capability" test. Software counts as a recording "device." A clause making the customer get consent did not protect AWS. No later ruling found (verify). | If vendor terms let it train on call content, the vendor can be a third-party eavesdropper. Offer a no-training option and a disclosure that names the vendor. | [CourtListener](https://www.courtlistener.com/opinion/10245579/gladstone-v-amazon-web-services-inc/) |
| *Taylor v. ConverseNow Technologies* | N.D. Cal., No. 25-cv-00990-SI; Aug 11, 2025 | A Domino's caller was routed without notice to an AI ordering assistant and gave her address and card data. The vendor allegedly used calls to improve its products. | Motion to dismiss denied under the capability test; §§ 631 and 632 claims proceed. A routine order with personal and payment data can be "confidential." No later ruling found (verify). | Disclose the AI vendor at the start of the call, get all-party consent, and limit the vendor's secondary use of audio. | [Privacy World](https://www.privacyworld.blog/2025/12/extra-large-pii-zza-courts-allows-california-privacy-class-action-to-proceed-for-use-of-ai-phone-call-assistant/); [Order](https://storage.courtlistener.com/recap/gov.uscourts.cand.443577/gov.uscourts.cand.443577.35.0.pdf) |
| *Turner v. Nuance Communications* | N.D. Cal., No. 22-cv-05827; May 28, 2024 | CIPA suit against a voice-AI vendor over processing of callers' voices (details: verify). | Applied the capability test: the vendor's ability to use data to improve its own products made it plausibly a third party. Final outcome: verify. | Vendor reuse of speech to improve models is what triggers liability. | [Cited in Gladstone](https://www.courtlistener.com/opinion/10245579/gladstone-v-amazon-web-services-inc/) |
| *Javier v. Assurance IQ* | 9th Cir., No. 21-16351 (May 31, 2022, unpublished); on remand N.D. Cal., 649 F. Supp. 3d 891 (2023) | A third-party tool recorded the user's session on an insurance-quote site. | The 9th Cir. held prior consent is required and later acceptance of a privacy policy does not cure (verify). The remand decision created the capability test later used in *Gladstone* and *Taylor*. | Get consent before capture starts, not at the end of the conversation. | [National Law Review](https://natlawreview.com/article/no-wire-no-violation-converses-cipa-win-affirmed-ninth-circuit-court-appeals); [Gladstone](https://www.courtlistener.com/opinion/10245579/gladstone-v-amazon-web-services-inc/) |
| *Yockey v. Salesforce* | N.D. Cal., No. 4:22-cv-09067-JST | Salesforce chat on Rite Aid and Kaiser sites; the vendor stored transcripts. | August 2024: CIPA § 632 and Pennsylvania wiretap (WESCA) claims survived; health chats counted as confidential. Sept 2026: class certification denied because the named plaintiff's chat revealed nothing sensitive (single secondary source, verify). | Sensitive content drives both confidentiality and standing. Limit what the bot captures and what the vendor can reuse. | [Bloomberg Law](https://news.bloomberglaw.com/litigation/consumers-advance-salesforce-class-action-over-intercepted-chats); [Salesforce Dictionary](https://salesforcedictionary.com/news/salesforce-news-september-6-2026-chat-wiretap-class-cert-denied) |
| *Jones v. Peloton Interactive* | S.D. Cal., No. 3:23-cv-01082; July 5, 2024 | Drift chatbot on the Peloton site. The amended complaint alleged Drift uses chat data for its own benefit. | First complaint dismissed Mar 2024. Amended § 631 claim survived. Later status: verify. | Allegations about the vendor's own use of data decide these cases. | [Bloomberg Law](https://news.bloomberglaw.com/litigation/peloton-privacy-class-action-proceeds-over-data-in-chat-feature) |
| *Jones v. Tonal Systems* | S.D. Cal., 751 F. Supp. 3d 1025 (2024) | Same plaintiff and same vendor (Drift chat). | Dismissed. The complaint alleged only that a vendor existed, so the party exemption applied. § 632.7 does not reach web chat. | Contract terms that confine the vendor to processing for the client help. | [Inside Class Actions](https://www.insideclassactions.com/2025/04/15/another-california-court-rejects-privacy-claims-targeting-online-chat-feature/) |
| *Licea v. Cinmar*; *Licea v. American Eagle* | C.D. Cal., both Mar 7, 2023 | Retail web-chat transcripts recorded by third-party software. | Dismissed on several grounds: party exemption, vendor as the site's "extension," no capture in transit, § 632.7 requires telephones. | The "tool" defense works only if the vendor has no independent rights to the data. | [Inside Class Actions](https://www.insideclassactions.com/2023/05/09/a-closer-look-courts-reject-california-wiretap-claims-based-on-website-chat-features/) |
| *Valenzuela v. Nationwide Mutual* | C.D. Cal., 686 F. Supp. 3d 969 (Aug 14, 2023) | Website chat routed through a third-party vendor (verify). | *Gladstone* groups it with decisions treating vendors as third parties simply because they are separate companies. Claim survived the pleadings (verify). | Some judges require no capability showing at all. | [Cited in Gladstone](https://www.courtlistener.com/opinion/10245579/gladstone-v-amazon-web-services-inc/) |
| *Mikulsky v. Bloomingdale's* | 9th Cir., 2025 WL 1718225 (June 20, 2025) | A session-replay vendor captured names, addresses, card data and product choices typed on the site. | Dismissal reversed. Real-time capture of typed personal data is "contents." | Any widget that streams what customers type to a vendor in real time is capturing content. | [National Law Review](https://natlawreview.com/article/split-signals-ninth-circuit-cipa-cases-against-converse-and-bloomingdales-give-rise) |
| *McGoveran v. Amazon Web Services and Pindrop* | 3d Cir., No. 24-3215; May 12, 2026 (affirming D. Del.) | Illinois residents called John Hancock. Calls ran on AWS servers in Virginia, and Pindrop matched voiceprints. | Affirmed. BIPA does not reach AWS because the conduct did not occur "primarily and substantially" in Illinois. Pindrop falls under BIPA's financial-institution exemption. A parallel Illinois state suit continues (verify). | Where voice processing and storage happen matters. Illinois state courts apply looser standing rules. | [3d Cir. opinion](https://cases.justia.com/federal/appellate-courts/ca3/24-3215/24-3215-2026-05-12.pdf); [Biometric Update](https://www.biometricupdate.com/202502/amazon-cant-block-bipa-voice-biometrics-case-even-though-federal-case-failed) |
| *Gundersen v. Amazon.com* (Alexa Voice ID) | N.D. Ill.; class certified, reported Nov 2025 (docket: verify) | About 1.18 million Illinois Voice ID enrollees. Prompts said "Voice ID," not "voiceprint" or "biometric identifier." | Class certified under BIPA § 15(b), (c) and (d). Whether Voice ID creates a voiceprint is left for trial. Appeal status: verify. | Voice enrollment needs a written release using BIPA's terms and a retention schedule. Record the user's location at enrollment. | [Duane Morris](https://blogs.duanemorris.com/classactiondefense/2025/11/26/the-class-action-weekly-wire-episode-128-illinois-federal-judge-certifies-class-of-1-2-million-amazon-alexa-users-in-bipa-class-action/) |

### 2.12.4 Telemarketing and AI voice

| Case | Court and date (with docket number if available) | Facts | Outcome or status | Lesson for virtual agents | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| *McLaughlin Chiropractic Assocs. v. McKesson Corp.* | U.S. Supreme Court, 606 U.S. 146 (June 2025) | Fax case that challenged an FCC interpretation of the TCPA. | District courts are not bound by FCC interpretations; they must read the statute themselves. | The FCC's 2024 ruling that AI voices are "artificial" voices is now persuasive only. Assume courts will still read the statute to cover AI voices. | [TCPAWorld](https://tcpaworld.com/2026/02/25/written-consent-new-fifth-circuit-decision-says-congress-never-required-it-in-the-first-place/) |
| *Bradford v. Sovereign Pest Control of TX* | 5th Cir., No. 24-20379; Feb 25, 2026 | Prerecorded renewal calls to a customer who gave his number on a service agreement. | The TCPA requires only "prior express consent," oral or written, even for telemarketing. The FCC's written-consent rule conflicts with the statute. | In the 5th Circuit, oral consent captured by an AI agent may suffice. Record and log it. | [TCPAWorld](https://tcpaworld.com/2026/02/25/written-consent-new-fifth-circuit-decision-says-congress-never-required-it-in-the-first-place/) |
| *Bradley v. DentalPlans.com* | D. Md., 2026 WL 788856 (Mar 20, 2026) | An inbound caller orally agreed to autodialed or prerecorded contact. | On reconsideration after *McKesson*: written consent not required. Consent must cover the technology used, but need not mention telemarketing. | Consent scripts should name the technology, for example "automated or AI-generated voice calls." | [TCPAWorld](https://tcpaworld.com/2026/03/23/massive-ruling-maryland-district-court-joins-fifth-circuit-in-holding-tcpa-does-not-require-written-consent-as-kay-fcc-created-robocall-protections-begin-to-erode/) |
| *Smith v. ExamWorks* | D. Md., 2026 WL 2823260 (Sept 21, 2026) | Prerecorded calls scheduling medical exams for GEICO claimants. | Class certification denied. The court suggested consent attaches to the call, not the technology (possibly dicta). This conflicts with *Bradley* in the same district. | The law is unsettled. Design consent for the stricter view. | [TCPAWorld](https://tcpaworld.com/2026/09/22/robocalls-allowed-anytime-a-number-is-provided-court-suggests-providing-number-to-a-caller-constitutes-tcpa-express-consent-for-robocalls-and-this-is-very-interesting/) |
| *Cruz v. United Collection Bureau* | S.D. Cal., 2026 WL 3062149 (Oct 13, 2026) | Debt-collection calls placed through LiveVox HCI. | Autodialer claim lost on summary judgment. Prerecorded-voice claim goes to a jury because the plaintiff said the calls sounded identical. | The artificial or prerecorded voice rule applies even without an autodialer. Keep per-call logs showing whether a bot spoke. | [TCPAWorld](https://tcpaworld.com/2026/10/15/prerecorded-problem-debt-collector-stuck-in-tcpa-suit-for-alleged-use-of-prerecorded-calls-even-though-no-atds-was-used/) |
| *Finley v. Altrua* | Court and docket not stated (verify); reported Nov 9, 2026 | AiRudder AI voice calls placed by Crown Administrators for Altrua during a nine-day campaign. | Class settlement of $1.1 million for about 900 recipients. | Even a short pilot of AI outbound calling creates class exposure. Get clear consent before any test. | [TCPAWorld](https://tcpaworld.com/2026/11/09/outbound-ai-void-leads-to-massive-tcpa-settlement-altrua-settles-tcpa-class-action-involving-airudder-contact-to-just-900-people-for-1-1mm/) |
| *Baus v. Allstate, Hardin Agency and InboundCD* | W.D. Wash., No. 2:26-cv-04337; filed Nov 2026 | AI voice lead-generation calls were warm-transferred to an insurance agency. A human agent called them "AI prerecorded." Calls continued after the consumer complained. | Newly filed putative class action under TCPA § 227(b) and Washington's Commercial Electronic Mail Act. | The brand and the agent receiving transfers are sued with the vendor. Opt-outs must reach every partner. | [National Law Review](https://natlawreview.com/article/not-again-allstate-facing-tcpa-class-action-over-alleged-ai-voice-calls-used-hardin) |

Settlements are turning into licenses. | [Tracker](https://chatgptiseatingtheworld.com/category/suno/) |

### 2.12.8 Sanctions for AI-generated errors

**Charlotin AI Hallucination Cases database** (page "Last updated: 25 November 2026"): 2,338 decisions worldwide; 1,585 in the USA; 253 Canada; 113 Australia; 91 UK. By filer: 1,315 pro se litigants, 913 lawyers, 34 judges. ([Database](https://www.damiencharlotin.com/hallucinations/))

| Case | Court and date (with docket number if available) | Facts | Outcome or status | Lesson for virtual agents | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| *Johnson v. Dunn* (Butler Snow) | N.D. Ala.; July 23, 2025 | ChatGPT produced four fake citations in filings by a large firm. | Public reprimand, disqualification from the case, bar referral. Correct underlying law did not excuse the fake sources. | Being "right anyway" does not excuse an invented source. | [Charlotin](https://www.damiencharlotin.com/hallucinations/177-johnson-v-dunn/) |
| *Coomer v. Lindell* | D. Colo.; July 7, 2025 | Fake and misquoted cases in an opposition brief. | $3,000 for each of two lawyers. The same lawyers were later sanctioned $4,500 in *Pelishek v. City of Sheboygan* (E.D. Wis., Sept 18, 2025). | Repeat errors look intentional. Fix the root cause, not just one output. | [Charlotin](https://www.damiencharlotin.com/hallucinations/16-coomer-v-lindellmypillow-inc-1/); [Pelishek](https://www.damiencharlotin.com/hallucinations/371-pelishek-v-city-of-sheboygan/) |
| *Wadsworth v. Walmart* (Morgan & Morgan) | D. Wyo.; Feb 24, 2025 | The firm's in-house AI tool invented 8 of 9 citations. | Drafting lawyer fined $3,000 and removed from the case; $1,000 each for two signing lawyers. | An in-house tool is no shield. Whoever signs off must check. | [Charlotin](https://www.damiencharlotin.com/hallucinations/4-wadsworth-v-walmart-morgan-morgan/) |
| *Gauthier v. Goodyear Tire & Rubber* | E.D. Tex.; Nov 25, 2024 | Claude produced two fake cases and seven fake quotations. | $2,000 penalty, AI training course, order disclosed to the client. | Checking with another AI tool did not count as verification. | [Charlotin](https://www.damiencharlotin.com/hallucinations/17-gauthier-v-goodyear-tire-rubber-co/) |
| *Gill v. Everyday Dose* | D. Del., November 9, 2026 | Lawyer; fabricated AI citations (database listing; decision not opened) | USD 6,000 and a bar referral (T2) | Verify every citation an AI tool produces | [Database](https://www.damiencharlotin.com/hallucinations/) |
| *Lingle v. Subaru of Wichita* | Kan., November 20, 2026 | Pro se litigant; AI-fabricated authority (database listing) | USD 6,680 (T2) | Sanctions apply to pro se filers too | [Database](https://www.damiencharlotin.com/hallucinations/) |
| *Gardner v. Coldwell Banker* | D.N.M., November 10, 2026 | Pro se litigant; AI-fabricated authority (database listing) | USD 1,000 (T2) | Same as above | [Database](https://www.damiencharlotin.com/hallucinations/) |
| *Noland v. Land of the Free* | Cal. Ct. App.; Sept 12, 2025 | 21 of 23 quotations in the opening brief were fabricated. | $10,000 sanction; State Bar notified. | Fake quotations from real sources are as serious as fake sources. | [Charlotin](https://www.damiencharlotin.com/hallucinations/514-noland-v-land/) |
| *Shahid v. Esaam* | Ga. Ct. App.; June 30, 2025 | A trial-court order drafted by counsel relied on fake cases. | Order vacated and case sent back; $2,500 penalty on the lawyer. | Errors pass through reviewers. A human approval step must actually check. | [Charlotin](https://www.damiencharlotin.com/hallucinations/235-shahid-v-esaam/) |
| *In re CorMedix* (Judge Neals, D.N.J.); *Jackson Federation of Teachers v. Fitch* (Judge Wingate, S.D. Miss.) | July 23 and July 20, 2025 | Judicial orders contained AI-generated errors. | Both orders withdrawn. The judges blamed an intern and a clerk in letters to Sen. Grassley. The 5th Cir. later reviewed the Wingate incident (No. 25-60496, verify). | Silent corrections draw criticism. Log changes and disclose corrections. | [Charlotin: CorMedix](https://www.damiencharlotin.com/hallucinations/311-in-re-cormedix/); [Charlotin: Fitch](https://www.damiencharlotin.com/hallucinations/331-jackson-federation-of-teachers-et-al-v-lynn-fitch/) |
| *N.Z. v. Fenix International*; *U.S. ex rel. Phillips v. L.A. Film School*; *Salgado v. Fred Palla Farms* | C.D. Cal. (Fenix order Dec 2025); C.D. Cal., Oct 23, 2026; Cal. Ct. App., Nov 6, 2026 | Briefs with AI-generated fake citations. | *Fenix*: $13,000 plus bar referral. *Phillips*: $5,000, training, bar referral. *Salgado*: $5,000 plus bar referral. | Bar referrals are now routine. Ground and check anything a bot says about law or policy. | [Charlotin: Fenix](https://www.damiencharlotin.com/hallucinations/504-nz-et-al-v-fenix-international-ltd-et-al-onlyfans/); [Charlotin: Phillips](https://www.damiencharlotin.com/hallucinations/2913-united-states-of-america-et-al-v-los-angeles-film-school-llc-et-al/); [Charlotin: Salgado](https://www.damiencharlotin.com/hallucinations/2931-salgado-v-fred-palla-farms-inc/) |
