| DoD "unbiased AI" procurement templates (October 2026) | Guidance; compliance with EO 14319 and M-26-04 mandatory | Model cards, acceptable-use policies and reporting mechanisms in DoD LLM contracts |

### 3.10.4 Criminal justice and tax data

| Regime | Status | What it means for virtual agents |
|---|---|---|
| **FBI CJIS Security Policy** (version 6.1, effective June 2026) | In force; no AI-specific section | Any agent, model or log that touches criminal justice information must meet all CJIS controls (multi-factor authentication, encryption, audit logging, personnel screening, state CJIS Systems Agency approval); there is no AI exemption |
| **IRS Publication 1075** (federal tax information) | In force (Rev. 11-2021); a Rev. 12-2026 draft adds an AI definition and a new AI section (see Section 3.11.1) | Applies to federal, state and local agencies and contractors that receive federal tax information. Treat prompts, transcripts, retrieval stores, logs and outputs containing such information as covered (vendor interpretation; confirm with the IRS Office of Safeguards and update the Safeguard Security Report). The IRS's own generative AI policy (February 2026) bars entering sensitive taxpayer data into generative AI tools |

### 3.10.5 State and local government

| Item | Status | What it means |
|---|---|---|
| **GovRAMP** (formerly StateRAMP) | AI Security Task Force in its 2026 implementation phase: AI to be treated as a significant change notification, an AI self-reporting addendum (v1.0 published July 2026, T1), AI flags on the authorized-product list, and draft procurement guidance for AI-enabled products (planned first half of 2026, verify) | Disclose AI features and model changes to GovRAMP and state customers |
| State adoption of GovRAMP | Georgia: GovRAMP-aligned assessments for new cloud contracts from October 1, 2026 and verification from July 1, 2027; Arizona: renewals must include GovRAMP- or FedRAMP-aligned assessments from July 1, 2026 | Plan GovRAMP status for state sales |
| TX-RAMP (Texas) and other state programs | In force; AI-specific requirements not found | Check Texas DIR and each state's program |
| State AI laws for government use | For example Texas TRAIGA disclosure duties for government AI interactions; state and city government phone-line bills (Section 1.9.3) | Disclosure and human-option requirements for public-sector agents |

### 3.10.6 UK and EU public sector

| Item | Status | What it means |
|---|---|---|
| **UK Algorithmic Transparency Recording Standard (ATRS)** | Mandatory for central government departments (scope and exemptions policy December 17, 2024); encouraged for the wider public sector | Government bodies must publish a transparency record for algorithmic tools, including virtual agents, that affect the public |
| **UK AI Playbook for Government** (February 2025) | Guidance (10 principles; not legislation) | Expectations for safe, lawful and transparent AI in public services |
| UK G-Cloud, Technology Code of Practice, NCSC Cloud Security Principles (background) | In force for central-government buying | Security and procurement evidence for agent vendors |
| **EU Model Contractual Clauses for AI procurement (MCC-AI)** | Updated March 5, 2025 (high-risk and non-high-risk versions); voluntary | Standard contract terms public buyers use to pass AI Act duties to vendors |
| EU AI Act duties for public bodies | Applying in stages | Public authorities deploying high-risk AI must complete fundamental-rights impact assessments and register use in the EU database |

### 3.10.7 Checklist for virtual agents in government

| Requirement | Source | Evidence or test (catalog IDs) |
|---|---|---|
| Authorization boundary covers every component, including the model provider at the same or a higher impact level | FedRAMP, DISA SRG, GovRAMP | Boundary diagram; Marketplace listings |
| No training on agency data without authorization; data stays in the authorized environment and region | FedRAMP AI Prioritization criteria; M-25-22 | Contract terms; retention and deletion tested (Q3); K8 covers data residency |
| Model, version, prompt and tool changes handled as significant changes | FedRAMP significant change notifications; GovRAMP AI notification | Change log; safety regression before each change (K3) |
| AI-specific continuous monitoring | FedRAMP 20x KSIs; M-25-21 ongoing monitoring | Drift, jailbreak, refusal and incident metrics (N2) |
| Pre-deployment testing and impact assessment for high-impact uses | M-25-21 | Corner-case catalog results; ISO/IEC 42005-style assessment |
| Human oversight, remedy and appeal for adverse outcomes | M-25-21 | F7, M5 |
| Unbiased AI documentation: acceptable-use policy, model or system card, user reporting of problematic outputs | EO 14319; M-26-04 | A working "report this answer" path that reaches the vendor and agency |
| Enterprise identity: single sign-on, SCIM, role-based access | FedRAMP AI Prioritization | K5 |
| Accessibility | Section 508; ADA Title II | O1 to O5 |
| Privacy documentation and records retention | Privacy Act; Federal Records Act | L5; retention schedule for transcripts |
| Criminal-justice and tax data never sent to unapproved model endpoints | CJIS; IRS Publication 1075 | Data-flow tests modeled on L1 to L4 |
| Public AI disclosure and transparency records | ATRS (UK); state laws; agency policy | M1; published transparency record |
| Incident reporting to the agency and FedRAMP | FedRAMP incident communications procedures | Incident drill |

## 3.11 IRS rules, Publication 1075 and other federal and state data regulations

Virtual agents that answer tax, benefits, child-support, health-exchange, student or insurance questions often handle data covered by regimes that predate AI but apply fully to it. None of these regimes exempts AI: prompts, retrieval stores, transcripts, call recordings, logs, model fine-tuning sets and outputs that contain covered data are covered data. This section adds detail to the short CJIS and Publication 1075 entries in Section 3.10.4.

### 3.11.1 IRS Publication 1075 (federal tax information)

**What it is.** *Tax Information Security Guidelines for Federal, State and Local Agencies* turns the safeguarding conditions of IRC § 6103(p)(4) into physical, personnel and technical controls built on NIST SP 800-53 Rev. 5. It applies to every federal, state, tribal and local agency that receives federal tax information (FTI), for example revenue, child-support, human-services, benefits and workforce agencies, and to their contractors, subcontractors and cloud providers. The IRS Office of Safeguards reviews compliance; TIGTA investigates unauthorized access and disclosure. Information derived from FTI is still FTI.

**Status.**

| Edition | Status | AI-relevant content |
|---|---|---|
| **Rev. 11-2021** | **In force** (the binding text as of November 2026) | No AI section. All controls apply to AI systems that store, process or transmit FTI |
| **Rev. 12-2026 (early-release draft)** | **Pending**; posted on irs.gov marked as a draft; takes effect six months after final publication; comments to the Office of Safeguards | Adds a definition of **"Artificial Intelligence"** ("a machine-based system that can, for a given set of human-defined objectives, make predictions, recommendations, or decisions influencing real or virtual environments") and of **cloud service provider**; adds a **new Section 3.3.9, Artificial Intelligence**, on the use of FTI within AI systems (text of 3.3.9 not reviewed here; verify); updates Exhibit 2 to align with OMB M-25-22 (reported); expands Section 3.3.1 cloud computing and Section 2.E.6.1 (the agency must show legal protections for FTI are in place and explain how it monitors the cloud provider); prohibits **personal-assistant smart devices** in FTI workspaces (AC-20 CE-3); requires quarterly phishing simulations (AT-2 CE-1); adds Taxpayer First Act § 2004 contractor certification and review requirements; accepts FBI Next Generation Identification enrollment for reinvestigation; removes AC-23 (data mining protection) and several other controls; says Secure Data Transfer was retired in December 2025 (reported) |

**Requirements that matter most for virtual agents (Rev. 11-2021 unless noted).**

| Requirement | Value | What it means for a virtual agent |
|---|---|---|
| Safeguard Security Report (SSR) | Approved at least 90 days before receiving FTI; updated annually on the agency group's due date; Head of Agency certification | List the agent, its model endpoints, speech services, retrieval stores and logging in the SSR and data-flow diagram; update it when the AI stack changes |
| 45-day notification | To the Office of Safeguards at least 45 days before a contractor agreement that gives access to FTI, and before moving FTI to cloud and other notifiable technologies | Adding an AI vendor, model provider, contact-center platform or new cloud region that can reach FTI needs notice first; never "move first, notify later" |
| US-only processing | FTI may be processed and stored only in the United States and its territories (SA-9); contractor staff and systems certified as US-located | Model inference, transcription, human review and support must all stay in the US; check the model provider's regions, fallback routing and offshore support |
| Incident reporting | Possible unauthorized inspection or disclosure reported immediately, no later than 24 hours after identification, to TIGTA and the Office of Safeguards, without waiting for an internal investigation | A bot that reads FTI to the wrong caller, a prompt-injection leak or a transcript sent to an unapproved tool is a reportable incident |
| Data breach definition (draft Rev. 12-2026) | Includes an **oral disclosure** of FTI to a person not authorized to receive it and an authorized user accessing FTI for an unauthorized purpose | Voice agents must authenticate the caller and confirm authority before speaking any FTI; misheard or spoofed identities are breach scenarios |
| Multi-factor authentication | All privileged and non-privileged accounts that can reach FTI at NIST SP 800-63 AAL2 (IA-2) | Agent admin consoles, prompt and knowledge-base editors and analytics dashboards need MFA |
| Service accounts and API keys | Least privilege (AC-6), account management (AC-2), audit (AU-6) | The agent's own credentials to FTI systems need an owner, scope limited to the needed data, vaulted secrets, rotation and logging |
| Authorized access list and need to know | Reviewed monthly | Human reviewers of transcripts and model outputs must be on the list |
| Background investigations | For employees and contractors with FTI access; reinvestigation every 5 years | Applies to vendor staff who can see prompts, logs or recordings |
| Training | Annual disclosure awareness training; security awareness updates at least quarterly | Include AI-specific misuse (pasting FTI into public chatbots) |
| Encryption | FIPS-validated cryptography in transit and at rest (SC-8, SC-13, SC-28) | Applies to vector stores, caches and recordings |
| Contract language | Exhibit 7 safeguarding language in every contract; may be expanded but not reduced | Put Exhibit 7 into AI and contact-center vendor contracts, with flow-down to sub-processors |
| End of contract | Contractor returns or destroys FTI within 7 calendar days | Includes fine-tuning data, embeddings, caches and backups held by the AI vendor |
| Logs and records | FTI logs kept 5 years | Retention schedule for agent transcripts that contain FTI |
| Commingling and labeling | FTI must be identifiable; derived data remains FTI; masking does not remove the obligation | Model outputs and summaries built from FTI are FTI |
| FedRAMP | FedRAMP authorization helps but is not by itself sufficient; Publication 1075 parameters and attestations still apply (vendor interpretation) | Confirm the model provider's contract covers Publication 1075 obligations |

**Penalties (apply to individuals as well as organizations).**

| Provision | Conduct | Maximum penalty |
|---|---|---|
| **IRC § 7213** | Willful unauthorized disclosure of returns or return information | Felony: fine up to $5,000 and up to 5 years' imprisonment, plus prosecution costs; federal employees also dismissed (background; verify current amounts against 26 U.S.C. § 7213) |
| **IRC § 7213A** | Willful unauthorized inspection ("browsing") | Misdemeanor: fine up to $1,000 and up to 1 year's imprisonment (background; verify) |
| **IRC § 7431** | Knowing or negligent unauthorized inspection or disclosure | Civil damages to the taxpayer: the greater of $1,000 per act or actual damages, plus punitive damages for willful or grossly negligent acts and costs (background; verify) |
| Administrative | Safeguard review findings | Corrective action plans; the IRS can suspend or terminate FTI disclosures to an agency that fails to safeguard |

### 3.11.2 Other IRS rules

| Rule | Who it applies to | Status | Relevance to virtual agents | Penalties |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **IRC § 6103** (confidentiality of returns) | IRS, agencies and contractors receiving FTI | In force | The legal basis for Publication 1075; limits who may receive FTI and for what purpose | §§ 7213, 7213A, 7431 (above) |
| **IRC § 7216 and Treas. Reg. § 301.7216** (use and disclosure by tax return preparers) | Tax preparers and anyone in the business of preparing returns, including software and online providers | In force; **no IRS AI-specific guidance under § 7216 found** (as of late 2026) | Using or disclosing return information outside the regulatory exceptions needs the taxpayer's signed consent in the required format (Rev. Proc. 2013-14, background). Commentators say sending client tax data to a third-party AI service is likely a "disclosure" unless an exception (such as the preparer's service-provider exception) applies; a general-purpose public chatbot probably does not qualify. Consent for marketing or non-preparation uses by a tax-help bot needs particular care | Criminal: misdemeanor, fine up to $1,000 and up to 1 year's imprisonment per violation (up to $100,000 for disclosures connected with identity theft) (background; verify) |
| **IRC § 6713** | Same as § 7216 | In force | Civil counterpart to § 7216 | $250 per unauthorized use or disclosure, up to $10,000 per year; higher for identity-theft-related disclosures (background; verify) |
| **OPR Alert 2026-19**, *Introductory Guidelines for Responsible AI Use in Federal Tax Practice* (June 24, 2026) | Practitioners under **Circular 230** (attorneys, CPAs, enrolled agents and others) | Guidance; applies existing Circular 230 duties, does not amend them | Cites due diligence (§ 10.22), fees (§ 10.27), competence (§ 10.35), procedures to ensure compliance (§ 10.36) and written advice (§ 10.37), plus confidentiality. Practitioners keep full responsibility for AI-assisted work, must review AI-drafted correspondence before sending, should use secure enterprise AI for client data, and should bill to reflect AI efficiencies. Not a ban on AI. The IRS also announced it will merge OPR and the Return Preparer Office (reported) | Circular 230 sanctions: censure, suspension or disbarment from practice before the IRS, and monetary penalties (background) |
| **FTC Safeguards Rule** (16 CFR Part 314, under GLBA) | Non-bank financial institutions, **including paid tax preparers** | In force; notification amendment effective May 13, 2024 | Written information security program, risk assessment, MFA, encryption, vendor oversight; report a "notification event" (unauthorized acquisition of unencrypted information of 500 or more consumers) to the FTC within 30 days of discovery | FTC enforcement; civil penalties where a rule violation is also covered by FTC civil-penalty authority (verify) |
| **IRS Publication 4557** (*Safeguarding Taxpayer Data*) and **Publication 5708** (WISP template) | Tax professionals | Guidance implementing the Safeguards Rule for preparers | Practical controls and a written information security plan; AI tools that touch client data belong in the plan. PTIN renewal includes an attestation to having a security plan (background; verify) | Through the Safeguards Rule and IRS e-file sanctions |
| **IRS Publication 1345** (*Handbook for Authorized IRS e-File Providers*) | Electronic return originators, transmitters and online providers | In force | Online providers must meet six security, privacy and business standards (background; verify current list) | Sanctions up to expulsion from IRS e-file |
| **IRS Publication 4812** (*Contractor Security Controls*, Rev. 13, 2022 latest found) | Contractors and subcontractors with access to IRS sensitive but unclassified (SBU) information or systems | In force | AI and contact-center vendors working for the IRS itself (not for states) follow Publication 4812 rather than Publication 1075 | Contract remedies plus §§ 7213, 7213A, 7431 |
| **Taxpayer First Act § 2004** | Contractors receiving FTI | In force; reflected in the draft Publication 1075 | Contractor certification and review requirements | Contract and disclosure-authority consequences |
| **IRM 10.24.1** (*IRS Policy for AI Governance*) and IRS generative AI policy | IRS employees, developers and AI providers to the IRS | Interim guidance (March 11, 2025) then IRM update reported February 2026 | AI risk assessments; bars entering sensitive taxpayer data into unapproved generative AI tools; addresses unintended disclosure from AI models. TIGTA (2024) and GAO (GAO-26-107522) found gaps in IRS AI risk management | Internal discipline; §§ 7213 and 7213A |

### 3.11.3 Other federal data regulations

| Regulation | Who it applies to | Status | Relevance to virtual agents | Maximum penalties |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **SSA data exchange security requirements** (Technical System Security Requirements, TSSR; information exchange agreements) | State agencies and their contractors that receive SSA data (benefits, Medicaid, SNAP, child support) | In force; latest TSSR found is version 8.50 (2019), agreements require the "most current" version (verify); SSA moved data exchanges to a new policy division in 2026 | SSA certifies partners before data flows and runs triennial security reviews; agreements usually also require Publication 1075 for tax data. Treat agent prompts, logs and recordings containing SSA data as covered; get SSA approval before routing it to a new AI vendor | Loss of data access; Privacy Act and Computer Matching penalties (below) |
| **Privacy Act of 1974 and Computer Matching and Privacy Protection Act** | Federal agencies and contractors operating systems of records; matching agreements with states | In force | Systems-of-records notices, routine-use limits, accounting of disclosures; an agent that queries records needs a lawful routine use | Criminal misdemeanor fines up to $5,000 for willful disclosure; civil actual damages (minimum $1,000) (background) |
| **CMS ARC-AMPE** (*Acceptable Risk Controls for ACA, Medicaid and Partner Entities*) | Federal and state-based health exchanges, Medicaid agencies, enhanced direct-enrollment entities and partners | In force; replaced MARS-E 2.2 and the NEE framework effective on publication (version 1.02, April 2025; later 1.03) | NIST SP 800-53 Rev. 5-based controls for systems handling exchange and Medicaid data, including federal tax data used for eligibility | Loss of connection to the federal data services hub; HIPAA and Publication 1075 penalties where applicable |
| **42 CFR Part 2** (substance-use-disorder treatment records) | Federally assisted SUD programs and recipients of their records | Final rule in force; compliance required by **February 16, 2026**; HHS OCR enforcing | Consent, redisclosure notices and breach notification now aligned with HIPAA; a health bot must not reveal or reuse SUD records outside consent | HIPAA civil tiers (up to about $2.19 million per year for identical violations in 2026) and criminal penalties now apply |
| **FERPA** (education records) and **PPRA** | Schools and institutions receiving federal education funds and their vendors acting as "school officials" | In force | Student-facing bots and AI tutors need a school-official agreement, direct control and no redisclosure | Loss of federal funding (rarely used); investigations by the Student Privacy Policy Office (background) |
| **COPPA Rule** (amended 2025) | Operators of services directed to children under 13 or with actual knowledge | In force; amended rule compliance date April 22, 2026 (verify) | Verifiable parental consent, separate consent for disclosure to third parties (including AI training), data minimization and retention limits | FTC civil penalties up to $53,088 per violation (Section 1.4 penalty table) |
| **Driver's Privacy Protection Act** | State DMVs and anyone obtaining motor-vehicle records | In force | Agents using DMV data for identity verification must have a permitted use | Civil liquidated damages of at least $2,500 per violation, plus punitive damages; criminal fines (background) |
| **FISMA and NIST SP 800-53 Rev. 5** | Federal agencies and contractors operating federal systems | In force | Baseline for FedRAMP, Publication 1075, ARC-AMPE and TSSR | Through contracts, ATOs and inspector general reporting |
| **NIST SP 800-171, DFARS 252.204-7012 and the proposed FAR CUI rule** | Contractors handling controlled unclassified information (CUI) | DFARS clause in force (requires Rev. 2); DFARS assessment clauses replaced by 252.240-7997 from February 1, 2026 and a further class deviation revision on September 3, 2026 (sources conflict on scope; verify); **FAR CUI rule re-proposed June 23, 2026** (Rev. 3 baseline for civilian contracts, 72-hour incident reporting), finalization expected around the end of 2026 | FTI is often marked CUI; AI tools and contact-center platforms that handle CUI fall under these controls | Contract remedies, False Claims Act exposure for false cyber-compliance representations (background) |
| **FBI CJIS Security Policy** | Criminal justice agencies and vendors | In force (version 6.1) | See Section 3.10.4 | Loss of CJIS access; state penalties |

### 3.11.4 State regulations

| Regulation | State | Status | Relevance to virtual agents | Maximum penalties |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **State tax confidentiality laws** | All states with income or sales taxes | In force | Most mirror IRC § 6103 for state tax data; state revenue bots need the same authentication, logging and vendor controls as for FTI, and state FTI agreements usually adopt Publication 1075 | Typically criminal penalties for unauthorized disclosure, with dismissal for employees (amounts vary; background) |
| **NYDFS Cybersecurity Regulation (23 NYCRR Part 500)** | New York (banks, insurers, money transmitters and other licensees) | In force; final amended requirements (universal MFA and asset inventory) effective **November 1, 2025**; first annual certification under the full rule due April 15, 2026 | MFA for all remote and privileged access; asset inventory including AI tools; third-party service-provider oversight; 72-hour cybersecurity-event notice; the 2024 AI cybersecurity industry letter (Section 3.3) applies these to AI-enabled social engineering and AI vendors | Penalties under the Financial Services and Banking Laws, assessed per violation; recent consent orders include $2.25 million (Delta Dental, April 30, 2026) and $2 million (PayPal, January 2025); one vendor reports more than $144 million across 27 entities (verify) |
| **CCPA regulations on automated decision-making technology (ADMT), risk assessments and cybersecurity audits** | California | Effective **January 1, 2026**; ADMT obligations from **January 1, 2027**; risk assessments for existing processing by December 31, 2027 with first submissions April 1, 2028; first cybersecurity audit certifications from April 1, 2028 for businesses above $100 million revenue (later for smaller ones) | Pre-use notice, opt-out and access rights for ADMT used in significant decisions; risk assessments for AI processing of personal information; audits of security programs | CCPA penalties of $2,663 per violation or $7,988 for intentional violations or those involving minors (Section 1.2 penalty table) |
| **NAIC Model Bulletin on the Use of AI Systems by Insurers** | About 24 states plus D.C. (as of spring 2026; one vendor reports 25 with 8 in progress; verify) | Guidance adopted by state insurance regulators; NAIC AI evaluation tool piloted in 12 states through September 2026 | Written AI program, governance, risk management, third-party vendor oversight and testing for unfair discrimination; applies to insurer chatbots and claims or underwriting agents | Enforcement under existing state unfair-trade-practice and market-conduct laws |
| **NAIC Insurance Data Security Model Law (#668)** | Adopted in many states (count unconfirmed; verify) | In force where adopted | Information security program, vendor oversight and 72-hour notice to the commissioner | State insurance penalties (vary) |
| **Massachusetts 201 CMR 17.00** | Massachusetts (anyone holding residents' personal information) | In force | Written information security program, encryption, vendor contracts | Enforced under Chapter 93A: up to $5,000 per violation (background) |
| **State breach-notification laws** | All 50 states, D.C. and territories | In force | Misdirected bot responses, transcript leaks and prompt-injection exfiltration can trigger notice; many states cover account credentials and biometric data | Varies, commonly per-violation or per-resident amounts with caps (for example up to $500,000 per breach in some states; background) |
| **Texas HB 3512** and **Texas Administrative Code Chapter 202 / TX-RAMP** | Texas state agencies and their cloud vendors | HB 3512 effective September 1, 2025 (annual state-certified AI training for employees and officials who use computers for at least 25% of their duties); TX-RAMP certification for cloud products; Texas TRAIGA duties for government AI (Section 1.2) | AI training and certification records for staff who manage agents; TX-RAMP certification for the agent platform | Contract and procurement consequences; TRAIGA penalties (Section 1.2) |
| **Utah SB 260 (2025) and SB 275 (2026), State-Endorsed Digital Identity** | Utah | SB 275 in force May 6, 2026; first credentials not yet issued (as of September 2026) | Agents that verify identity must allow physical ID, use selective disclosure and minimal attributes, avoid tracking, and meet the Duty of Loyalty as verifiers or relying parties; government systems must accept SEDI within three months of first issuance (Section 1.2, Utah) | Attorney general civil actions (injunctions, restitution, disgorgement, damages); no private right of action |
| **California SB 896** (Generative AI Accountability Act, 2024) | California state agencies | In force | Agencies must tell people when they are interacting with generative AI in state communications and services, inventory GenAI uses and assess risks, including to critical infrastructure | No private penalties found (verify) |
| **State CJIS, Medicaid and benefits agreements** | All states | In force | State agencies often add Publication 1075, TSSR and ARC-AMPE terms to vendor contracts in one agreement | Contract remedies; loss of federal data access |

### 3.11.5 Checklist for virtual agents that handle tax and regulated government data

| Requirement | Source | Evidence or test (catalog IDs) |
|---|---|---|
| Caller identity and authority confirmed before any FTI, SSA, SUD or student data is spoken or shown | IRC § 6103; Publication 1075 (oral disclosure as breach in the draft); 42 CFR Part 2; FERPA | Authentication and social-engineering tests, including spoofed caller ID and third-party "helper" scenarios; government ID handling (L4) |
| AI stack listed in the SSR and data-flow diagram; 45-day notice before new AI vendors or regions | Publication 1075 | SSR change log; IRS acknowledgement letter |
| All inference, transcription, storage, support and human review in the US | Publication 1075 SA-9 | Region pinning and fallback-routing tests; vendor attestation |
| No FTI or regulated data sent to unapproved model endpoints or public tools | Publication 1075; IRS generative AI policy; § 7216; Circular 230 alert | Data-loss and egress tests (L1 to L4); retention and deletion tested (Q3) |
| Written § 7216 consent before tax-preparer bots use or disclose return information for anything beyond preparation | § 7216; Treas. Reg. § 301.7216 | Consent capture and refusal-path tests |
| Practitioner review of AI-drafted tax advice and correspondence | Circular 230; OPR Alert 2026-19 | Human approval step logged before sending |
| MFA at AAL2 for agent administration, knowledge-base editing and transcript access | Publication 1075 IA-2; NYDFS Part 500; FTC Safeguards Rule | Access review (K5) |
| Agent service accounts scoped, vaulted, rotated and logged | Publication 1075 AC-2, AC-6, AU-6 | Credential inventory; misuse tests |
| 24-hour incident escalation path to TIGTA and the Office of Safeguards; 30-day FTC and 72-hour NYDFS paths where they apply | Publication 1075; FTC Safeguards Rule; NYDFS Part 500 | Tabletop exercise with an AI-leak scenario |
| Transcripts, embeddings and caches treated as covered records: labeled, encrypted, retained per schedule and destroyed within 7 days of contract end | Publication 1075 | Retention and destruction evidence from the AI vendor |
| No smart-speaker or personal voice assistants in FTI workspaces | Publication 1075 draft AC-20 CE-3 | Facility policy and inspection |
| Identity verification offers a physical-ID path, asks only for needed attributes, prefers yes-or-no proofs and keeps no tracking record | Utah SB 275 (SEDI); NIST SP 800-63 | Identity-verification flow tests with and without a digital wallet; data-minimization review (L4) |
| AI disclosure to the public in state services | California SB 896; Texas TRAIGA; state laws (Section 1.2) | AI disclosure tests (M1) |
| ADMT notices, opt-outs and risk assessments for significant decisions | CCPA regulations | Notice and opt-out tests; risk-assessment record |

## 3.12 OWASP AI and application security standards

OWASP projects are community standards, not law. They matter for compliance because regulators, auditors and contracts use them as the reference for "reasonable" AI security. Examples: the EU AI Act's robustness and cybersecurity duties (Article 15), PCI DSS secure-development requirements (Requirement 6), FedRAMP and agency ATO testing, NYDFS Part 500 and state "reasonable security" laws, and customer security questionnaires. OWASP's GenAI Security Project publishes a crosswalk to NIST, MITRE ATLAS, ISO and CWE.

### 3.12.1 OWASP projects relevant to virtual agents

| Project | Version and status | Scope | Use for a virtual agent |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Top 10 for LLM Applications 2026** | Released August 4, 2026 (Black Hat USA; announced in a September 2, 2026 press release); supersedes the 2025 list; CC BY-SA 4.0 | Risks where the model is a component inside an application. Ranking for the first time weighted partly (about 25%) by real incident data | Baseline threat model and test plan for every bot |
| **Top 10 for Agentic Applications 2026** (ASI01 to ASI10) | Published December 9, 2025 | Risks once a model plans, calls tools, holds memory or works with other agents | Required in addition to the LLM list for agents that take actions |
| **Agent Control Standard (ACS)** | Donated to the GenAI Security Project (announced September 2, 2026); maturity not confirmed | Controls for securing agentic systems | Candidate control set for agent permissions and oversight (verify content) |
| **GenAI Red Teaming Guide** (v1.0) and AI Testing Guide | Red Teaming Guide published; the AI Testing Guide is an open project still in development (sources conflict on a v1 release; verify) | Methods for adversarial testing of models, applications and agents | Structure for pre-production red teaming |
| **AIVSS** (AI Vulnerability Scoring System) | Version 0.8 (March 19, 2026, reported); extends CVSS 4.0 with AI and agentic factors; not final | Scoring AI vulnerabilities | Severity scoring for findings in Part 4 of the test catalog |
| **MCP Top 10** | Beta (labeled 2025), not final | Risks in Model Context Protocol servers and clients, for example token and secret exposure (MCP01) and privilege escalation through scope creep (MCP02) | Agents that use MCP tool servers |
| GenAI Data Security Risks and Mitigations guide | Released March 2026 (reported) | Data risks across the AI lifecycle | Data-flow and retention reviews |
| AI Security Solutions Directory and framework crosswalk | Updated September 2026 | Tool landscape and mapping to NIST, ISO, MITRE ATLAS | Selecting controls and showing auditors coverage |
| **ASVS** (Application Security Verification Standard), **API Security Top 10 (2023)**, **Web Top 10** | Current releases (background; ASVS 5.0 released 2025, verify) | Conventional web, API and identity security | The widget, APIs, authentication and admin consoles around the bot |

### 3.12.2 Top 10 for LLM Applications 2026

| ID | Risk | Change from 2025 | Virtual-agent example | Catalog tests |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **LLM01:2026** | Prompt injection | Still #1 | A caller or a retrieved web page tells the bot to ignore its rules | D, J |
| **LLM02:2026** | Sensitive information disclosure | Still #2; scope expanded | Bot reveals another customer's data or a card number from history | B, J, L |
| **LLM03:2026** | Excessive agency | Up from #6 (largest move) | Bot issues a refund or changes an address without verification or limits | I, B |
| **LLM04:2026** | Supply chain | Down from #3 | Compromised model, plugin, SDK or prompt template | K |
| **LLM05:2026** | Data and model poisoning | Down from #4 | Poisoned fine-tuning data or knowledge-base articles | J, A |
| **LLM06:2026** | Unbounded consumption | Up from #10; now includes "denial of wallet" | Scripted chats that run up inference cost or exhaust quotas | N, D |
| **LLM07:2026** | Misinformation | Up from #9 (pulled up by incident data) | Bot invents a refund policy (the Air Canada pattern) | A, G |
| **LLM08:2026** | Hidden context exposure | Renamed from System Prompt Leakage; now covers retrieved policy text, tool schemas and workflow rules | User extracts internal escalation rules or tool definitions | J |
| **LLM09:2026** | Vector and embedding weaknesses | Down from #8 | Cross-tenant retrieval or poisoned embeddings in a RAG store | J, K |
| **LLM10:2026** | Improper output handling | Down from #5 | Bot output inserted unescaped into a CRM, email, SQL or the chat widget (XSS) | D, K |

The 2026 edition shifts the goal from "a model that cannot be fooled" to "an application that limits the damage when it is fooled": least-privilege tools, human approval for consequential actions, treating all model output as untrusted and assuming all context can be extracted.

### 3.12.3 Top 10 for Agentic Applications 2026

| ID | Risk | Virtual-agent example | Catalog tests |
|---|---|---|---|
| ASI01 | Agent goal hijack | Instructions hidden in an email, document or web page redirect the agent's objective | J, I |
| ASI02 | Tool misuse | Agent uses an authorized tool in an unintended or harmful way (bulk cancellations, mass messages) | I |
| ASI03 | Identity and privilege abuse | Agent acts with a shared service account or inherits a user's privileges beyond the task | B, K5 |
| ASI04 | Agentic supply chain vulnerabilities | Malicious tool server, plugin or agent card | K |
| ASI05 | Unexpected code execution | Agent generates and runs code or commands from user input | D, I |
| ASI06 | Memory and context poisoning | Long-term memory stores a false "fact" or instruction that affects later customers | C, J |
| ASI07 | Insecure inter-agent communication | Spoofed or unauthenticated messages between agents | K, I |
| ASI08 | Cascading failures | One agent's error propagates through a chain of agents or retries | N, I |
| ASI09 | Human-agent trust exploitation | Agent persuades a user or an employee to approve a harmful action | G, H, F |
| ASI10 | Rogue agents | Agent operates outside its intended scope or persists after it should stop | I, N |

(ASI03 to ASI06 names follow the published list as summarized by secondary sources; verify wording on genai.owasp.org.)

### 3.12.4 Where OWASP is referenced in compliance

| Regime | How OWASP is used |
|---|---|
| EU AI Act Article 15 and harmonized standards; Cyber Resilience Act | Evidence of resilience against prompt injection, data poisoning and adversarial inputs |
| PCI DSS v4.0.1 Requirement 6.2.4 | Software must address common attacks; OWASP lists are the usual reference for injection and output handling |
| FedRAMP, agency ATOs, NIST SP 800-53 SA-11 and the planned COSAIS overlays | Penetration and red-team testing scope |
| HIPAA Security Rule risk analysis; NYDFS Part 500; FTC "reasonable security" orders | Threat identification for AI channels |
| UK AI Cyber Security Code of Practice and ETSI EN 304 223 (background) | Baseline security principles for AI systems |
| Customer contracts and security questionnaires | Increasingly ask for OWASP LLM and Agentic Top 10 test results |

## 3.13 ADA and US accessibility regulations for virtual agents

Chatbots, voice bots and IVRs are part of the services a business or government offers, so they must be usable by people with disabilities: screen-reader and keyboard users, deaf and hard-of-hearing users, people with speech disabilities whose speech recognition fails, and people with cognitive disabilities. AI also creates new accessibility risks, such as streaming text that screen readers announce badly, voice-only flows with no text option, timeouts, CAPTCHAs and speech recognition that does not understand atypical speech.

### 3.13.1 Federal laws and rules

| Law or rule | Who it covers | Status | Requirements relevant to virtual agents | Penalties and remedies |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **ADA Title II and the DOJ web and mobile rule (28 CFR Part 35, Subpart H)** | State and local governments, including public schools, colleges, courts, transit and utilities | Rule published April 24, 2024 at **WCAG 2.1 Level AA**. **Interim final rule of April 20, 2026** (91 Fed. Reg. 20902) extended compliance by one year: **April 26, 2027** for entities serving 50,000 or more people and **April 26, 2028** for smaller entities and special districts; comments closed June 22, 2026; DOJ may propose substantive changes | Government chatbots, voice assistants, web chat widgets, apps and kiosks must meet WCAG 2.1 AA; limited exceptions (archived content, some third-party content, preexisting documents). The general Title II duty of equal access applies now, regardless of the extension | DOJ enforcement and private suits; injunctive relief, compensatory damages for intentional discrimination, attorney's fees |
| **ADA Title III** (public accommodations) | Businesses open to the public: retail, banking, hospitality, health care, travel, education | In force; **no DOJ web regulation**; DOJ's 2022 guidance points to WCAG; courts are split on whether online-only businesses are covered (for example the Ninth and Eleventh Circuits require a link to a physical place, while some other courts do not) | Accessible chat and voice channels and a usable alternative (for example a staffed phone line or text option) for people who cannot use the bot | **Private plaintiffs:** injunctions and attorney's fees only (no damages under Title III itself). **DOJ:** civil penalties up to **$118,225 for a first violation and $236,451 for a subsequent violation** (2025 amounts; DOJ did not adjust for 2026 because October 2025 CPI data were unavailable), plus damages to aggrieved persons |
| **ADA Title I** (employment) | Employers with 15 or more employees | In force; the EEOC removed its AI technical-assistance documents (including the 2022 ADA and AI guidance) in January 2025, but the statute is unchanged | AI interview bots, screening chatbots and assessments must offer reasonable accommodations, must not screen out people with disabilities who can do the job, and must not make disability-related inquiries before an offer. Employers remain liable for vendors' tools | EEOC and private suits: back pay, compensatory and punitive damages (capped by employer size at $50,000 to $300,000), injunctions, fees (background) |
| **Section 504 of the Rehabilitation Act; HHS web and mobile rule (45 CFR 84.84)** | Recipients of federal financial assistance; the HHS rule covers health and human-services recipients | HHS rule adopted WCAG 2.1 AA (2024). **Interim final rule of May 7, 2026** (published May 11, 2026) extended compliance to **May 11, 2027** (15 or more employees) and **May 10, 2028** (fewer than 15). Other agencies' Section 504 duties continue | Patient-portal chatbots, telehealth assistants, benefits bots and kiosks of hospitals, clinics, insurers and agencies receiving HHS funds. Duty to make reasonable modifications continues during the extension; private right of action remains | Loss of federal funds; private suits (injunctions, compensatory damages for intentional discrimination) |
| **Section 1557 of the Affordable Care Act** (2024 rule) | Health programs receiving federal funds | In force (background; parts litigated) | Accessible ICT and auxiliary aids; also prohibits discrimination through patient care decision support tools, including AI (45 CFR 92.210, compliance from May 1, 2025, background) | HHS OCR enforcement; private suits |
| **Section 508 of the Rehabilitation Act** | Federal agencies and their ICT vendors | In force; Revised 508 Standards incorporate WCAG 2.0 AA | Agency chatbots and contact-center AI must conform; vendors supply Accessibility Conformance Reports (VPAT) | Administrative complaints, procurement consequences |
| **Air Carrier Access Act (14 CFR Part 382)** | US and foreign airlines | In force | Accessible websites and kiosks; airline chatbots must not be the only way to get disability assistance (background) | DOT civil penalties (background) |
| **CVAA (21st Century Communications and Video Accessibility Act)** and **FCC rules** | Advanced communications services, equipment and video programming | In force | Voice and messaging services must be accessible; relay services (TRS) and real-time text (RTT) interoperability for people who are deaf or speech-disabled; voice bots should accept relay and RTT calls (background) | FCC forfeitures (background) |
| **Proposed: Online Accessibility Act (H.R. 9539)** | Would amend Title III | Introduced; not passed | Would require notice and a 60-day cure period and a DOJ complaint before suit | |

### 3.13.2 State accessibility laws

| Law | State | Relevance | Remedies |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Unruh Civil Rights Act** (Civil Code §§ 51, 52) | California | Any ADA violation is also an Unruh violation; reach to online-only businesses is disputed in the courts | **Statutory damages of at least $4,000 per violation**, plus attorney's fees |
| **Disabled Persons Act** | California | Similar to Unruh (alternative claim) | Minimum $1,000 per violation (background) |
| **New York State and City Human Rights Laws** | New York | Frequent venue for website and app suits; New York City law covers online services broadly (background) | Compensatory damages, civil penalties and fees |
| **Colorado HB 21-1110** and accessibility rules | Colorado | State and local government ICT must meet the state's technology accessibility rules (WCAG-based) | Statutory damages of $3,500 per violation, injunctions (background) |
| **Colorado HB 26-1263 (Chatbot Safety Act)** and draft rules | Colorado | A draft referenced **WCAG 2.2** for AI notices and chatbot communications (draft only; verify) | Attorney General |
| State procurement accessibility rules (for example Texas, Illinois IITAA, Minnesota) | Various | Public-sector purchasing of chat and voice platforms | Contract consequences |

### 3.13.3 Litigation and enforcement trends

- **Volume:** one count puts 2025 at 8,667 federal ADA Title III suits of all types, 3,117 of them over website accessibility (36%, up 27% from 2024); another tracker counted 3,948 federal and state website suits in 2025. Forecasts for 2026 run from about 5,500 to more than 6,000 digital-accessibility suits, and one tracker recorded 431 new web and app suits in October 2026 (counts differ by method; vendor sources).
- **Generative AI is increasing filings:** reports show a large rise in self-represented (pro se) ADA filings in 2025 drafted with AI tools.
- **Overlays do not prevent suits:** about 25% of 2025 website suits targeted sites that already had an accessibility widget installed.
- **E-commerce is the main target** (about 79% of digital suits in the first half of 2026, one tracker).
- **DOJ involvement:** DOJ has brought fewer Title III website cases but in February 2026 filed a statement of interest opposing a proposed class settlement over a retailer's website accessibility (Alcazar v. Fashion Nova, ADA and Unruh claims).
- **Chatbot-specific case law is thin:** no reported decision specifically on an inaccessible AI chatbot was found, but chat widgets and voice systems are part of the websites and services already being sued over, and AI hiring tools face disability claims (Section 2.5, including the ACLU complaint against Intuit and HireVue).

### 3.13.4 Accessibility standards

| Standard | Status | Use |
|---|---|---|
| **WCAG 2.1 Level AA** | Legal baseline for the DOJ Title II and HHS Section 504 rules | Minimum target for US public-sector and health virtual agents |
| **WCAG 2.2 Level AA** | Current W3C Recommendation (2023, background) | Best-practice target: focus visibility, accessible authentication (no cognitive tests), redundant entry, consistent help |
| WCAG 3.0 | Working draft (background) | Monitor |
| EN 301 549 | EU and UK procurement standard; includes two-way voice and real-time text | Voice and chat bots sold to EU or UK public bodies |
| Section 508 Standards | US federal ICT | Federal agency bots |
| WAI-ARIA Authoring Practices | Guidance | Live regions for streaming chat replies, dialog focus management |

### 3.13.5 Accessibility checklist for virtual agents

| Requirement | Source | Evidence or test (catalog IDs) |
|---|---|---|
| Chat widget meets WCAG 2.1 AA (target 2.2 AA): keyboard operable, visible focus, labels, contrast, resizable text, no keyboard trap | ADA Titles II and III; Section 504; Section 508 | Automated scan plus manual testing (O2) |
| Streaming replies announced correctly by screen readers (ARIA live regions, no flood of partial tokens) | WCAG 4.1.3; ADA | NVDA, JAWS, VoiceOver and TalkBack tests (O1) |
| Equivalent text alternative to voice-only flows and voice alternative to text-only flows; accepts relay (TRS) and RTT calls | ADA; CVAA and FCC rules | Deaf, hard-of-hearing and speech-disabled user paths (O3, E1) |
| Speech recognition works for atypical speech, or the bot offers keypad, text or human alternatives after failures | ADA (equal access); Title I for hiring bots | Dysarthria, stutter and accent tests; failure-to-escalate tests (E1; F3, F6) |
| No timeouts without warning and extension; no inaccessible CAPTCHA or cognitive tests for authentication | WCAG 2.2.1, 3.3.8 | Timeout and authentication tests (B, O) |
| Plain-language responses and a way to ask for simpler explanations | ADA (effective communication); WCAG 3.1.5 (AAA, advisory) | Readability tests (O5) |
| A staffed human channel reachable from the bot without penalty | ADA reasonable modification; California AB 1609 and other right-to-a-human laws (Section 1.9.3) | Handoff tests (F) |
| Hiring and assessment bots offer accommodations and avoid disability screen-outs | ADA Title I | Accommodation-request path tests; adverse-impact review |
| Accessibility Conformance Report (VPAT) and remediation plan for the bot | Section 508; Title II procurement; EN 301 549 | Current ACR covering the chat and voice components |
| Accessibility regression on every model, prompt or UI change | WCAG conformance maintained over time | Release gate (K3, O1 to O5) |

## 3.14 Messaging controls for AI SMS and email, mapped to tests

These controls apply to every outbound and inbound SMS and email flow. They map the rules in Section 1.11 to controls a virtual-agent team can test. Test IDs refer to Virtual_Agent_Corner_Case_Test_Catalog.md, version 1.1, which adds category Q. Keep the evidence listed in the second column for each control, because regulators and courts ask for it.

| Control | Evidence to keep | Rules and standards | Test IDs |
|---|---|---|---|
| Consent record for each channel | Consent wording, time, channel, source and the technology named | TCPA and FCC 24-17; PECR; ePrivacy Article 13; CASL; Spam Act 2003; POPIA Section 69; Colombia Law 2300 | M3, Q5 |
| Registry and suppression scrub before each send | Scrub log with timestamp and list version; fail-closed status | US state lists; Australia Do Not Call Register; Singapore DNC Registry; Colombia RNE; UK TPS and CTPS; India DLT; Reassigned Numbers Database | M3 |
| Send windows and frequency caps set by the recipient's location | Window and cap configuration; attempted and blocked sends | TCPA time rule; Colombia Law 2300; Australian calling hours; Brazilian rules (Section 1.10.8) | M3, M6 |
| Opt-out and revocation across channels | Revocation events, time to suppression, cross-channel proof | CAN-SPAM (10 business days); CASL; PECR; POPIA; Japan; Korea; FCC revocation order | M4, Q4 |
| AI disclosure at the start and on request | Stored opening lines per language and channel; disclosure test results | California B.O.T. Act and AB 2905; Utah; Maine; Colorado; Washington; EU AI Act Article 50(1); Korea AI Basic Act | M1 |
| Sender identity and authentication | Header and sender-ID configuration; SPF, DKIM and DMARC results; registered sender IDs | CAN-SPAM; CASL; Spam Act 2003; India DLT; Japan; POPIA Section 69(4); Gmail, Yahoo and Microsoft sender rules | Q1 |
| Synthetic-content labels and marks | Label or watermark configuration; metadata retention tests | EU AI Act Article 50(2); China labeling measures; Korea AI Basic Act | Q2 |
| Inbound message handling as untrusted data | Prompt-injection test results; tool-permission matrix | GDPR Articles 5, 6 and 25; ePrivacy Article 5; OWASP LLM Top 10 (Section 3.12) | J2, J4, J9, category I |
| Recording and listening notice for voice and chat | Notice text and recording state for each interaction | CIPA Sections 631, 632 and 632.7; Florida all-party consent; GDPR Article 13 | M2 |
| Automated decisions taken from messages | Decision log, explanation and human-review route | GDPR Article 22; UK DUAA Articles 22A to 22D (verify); Australia APPs 1.7 to 1.9 from December 10, 2026; Colorado SB 26-189 | M5 |
| Human route on request by text or email | Escalation logs for "human" requests received by SMS and email | California AB 1609; NYC proposed rule (Section 1.9.3); Brazil Decree 13.135/2026 | F3, F6 |
| Sensitive or minors' data in messages | Detection rules, retention settings and access logs | GDPR Article 9; HIPAA where applicable (Section 3.2); COPPA and state children's laws (Section 1.4) | H and L categories; Q3 |

## 3.15 Call recording consent by state and country

Recording and AI analysis of a call are separate acts from answering it. The table gives the consent model to plan for. Every row is T3 background unless the Section 1.2 row says otherwise, so confirm each state with counsel.

| Jurisdiction | Rule | What the bot must do | Test ID |
|---|---|---|---|
| California (Penal Code 632 and 632.7; CIPA) | All-party consent for confidential communications. AI listening or recording by a vendor can be interception (Section 2.17) | Give notice before recording or AI analysis starts; get consent or offer a route that avoids recording | M2 |
| Florida (Statutes 934.03) | All-party consent | Same as California | M2 |
| Illinois, Maryland, Massachusetts, New Hampshire, Pennsylvania, Washington | All-party consent (verify each state) | Same as California | M2 |
| Montana | Recording without the knowledge of all parties is unlawful, but a warning given to one party allows either side to record (MCA 45-8-213; Section 1.2) | Treat as all-party and give a warning at the start of every recorded call (verify with counsel) | M2 |
| Connecticut, Delaware, Nevada | All-party consent in some circumstances (verify) | Apply the stricter rule to calls that cross state lines | M2 |
| Most other US states | One-party consent (verify each state, including Texas, New York and Utah) | Notice is still good practice, and some vendor terms require it | M2 |
| Canada (Criminal Code section 184) | One-party consent for a party to a call; interception by a third party needs consent (verify) | Notice before recording and AI analysis; consent where a third party is involved | M2 |
| European Union and United Kingdom | No single rule. GDPR Article 13 notice and a lawful basis apply; national criminal laws restrict secret recording (verify each member state) | Notice at the start of the call; record the lawful basis | M2 |
| Australia | State and territory listening-device laws differ; some require all-party consent (verify) | Notice and consent where the state law requires it | M2 |

## 3.16 Voice biometrics for authentication

Voice authentication creates a biometric template. Several US states regulate it, and the GDPR treats biometric data used for identification as special-category data.

| Rule | Status | What it means | Possible penalties |
|---|---|---|---|
| Illinois Biometric Information Privacy Act (740 ILCS 14) | In force | Written release before a voiceprint is collected; a published retention and destruction schedule; private right of action | Liquidated damages per violation (USD 1,000 negligent; USD 5,000 intentional or reckless; verify) |
| Texas CUBI (Business and Commerce Code 503.001) | In force | Consent before capturing a biometric identifier; no sale; retention limits | Attorney General civil penalties up to USD 25,000 per violation (verify) |
| Washington biometric identifier law (RCW 19.375) | In force | Notice and consent for enrollment; no sale without consent | Attorney General enforcement (verify) |
| New York City biometric identifier law (Administrative Code 22-1201 et seq.) | In force (verify) | Commercial establishments must post notice when they collect biometric identifiers; enforced by the city (verify) | Civil penalties (verify) |
| GDPR Article 9 | In force | Biometric data processed to identify a person is special-category data. Needs an Article 9 condition (usually explicit consent) and a data protection impact assessment | Up to EUR 20 million or 4% |

Test: Q7 (enrollment and withdrawal).

## 3.17 Vendor and processor contract terms

Most AI virtual-agent exposure runs through vendors and model providers. The contract sets what they may do with customer data.

| Rule | Status | What it means | Possible penalties |
|---|---|---|---|
| GDPR Article 28 | In force | Written processor contract; sub-processor approval; deletion or return at the end of the contract | Up to EUR 20 million or 4% |
| EU-US Data Privacy Framework and standard contractual clauses | In force; the General Court upheld it September 3, 2025; appeal C-703/25 P pending at the CJEU (T2) | Transfers of customer data outside the EU need a transfer mechanism | Under GDPR Article 83 |
| CCPA and CPRA service-provider contracts | In force | The contract must restrict use and retention and include required terms (verify) | Per Section 1.2 |
| Training and secondary-use terms | Negotiated | No training on customer data, or secondary use, without authorization; retention limits; audit rights; indemnity for privacy breaches. The Gladstone and Taylor cases turned on data-use terms (Section 2.17) | Contract remedies |

Tests: Q9 (vendor data use) and Q3 (retention).

## 3.18 Incident-notification matrix

Notification clocks run from different triggers. Start the clock from the earliest trigger that applies. All rows are T3 background and must be checked against the text before use.

| Regime | Trigger | Clock | Notify |
|---|---|---|---|
| GDPR Article 33 | Personal data breach | 72 hours after awareness, where feasible | Supervisory authority; individuals if high risk (Article 34) |
| NIS2 Directive (EU 2022/2555) | Significant incident at an essential or important entity | Early warning within 24 hours; notification within 72 hours; final report within one month (verify) | National CSIRT or competent authority |
| DORA (EU 2022/2554) | Major ICT-related incident at a financial entity | Initial notice within 4 hours of classification and no later than 24 hours after detection (verify) | Competent authority |
| EU Cyber Resilience Act | Actively exploited vulnerability or severe incident in a product with digital elements | Reporting obligations from September 11, 2026 via the ENISA Single Reporting Platform (T1); main CRA obligations December 11, 2027 | ENISA and national CSIRT (verify) |
| EU AI Act Article 73 | Serious incident involving a high-risk AI system | Timing depends on severity; applies when the high-risk rules apply (December 2, 2027 under the Digital Omnibus; verify) | Market surveillance authority |
| US SEC cybersecurity rules (Form 8-K, Item 1.05) | Material cybersecurity incident at a public company | Four business days after a materiality determination (verify) | SEC |
| CISA CIRCIA final rule (critical infrastructure incidents and ransom payments) | 72-hour incident report and 24-hour ransom-payment report once effective; final rule at OMB since October 1, 2026 (T2) | Pending | CISA |
| HIPAA Breach Notification Rule (45 CFR 164.400 to 414) | Breach of unsecured protected health information | Without unreasonable delay and within 60 days of discovery | Individuals; HHS; media if 500 or more (verify) |
| NYDFS Part 500 | Cybersecurity event at a covered entity | 72 hours (verify) | NYDFS |
| IRS Publication 1075 | Incident involving federal tax information | Report to TIGTA and the IRS within 24 hours (verify) | TIGTA and IRS |
| US state breach laws | Personal information breach | Varies; some states require notice within 30 days (verify each) | Individuals and state regulators |
| Australia Notifiable Data Breaches (Privacy Act Part IIIC) | Eligible data breach | Assess within 30 days; notify as soon as practicable (verify) | OAIC and affected individuals |
| Brazil LGPD Article 48 | Security incident likely to cause relevant risk | Within a reasonable time set by ANPD rules (verify) | ANPD and data subjects |

## 3.19 Agent actions that move money or change accounts

When a virtual agent can refund, change an address or move funds, consumer-finance and agency rules apply to the agent's acts as if a person had done them.

| Rule | Status | What it means | Possible penalties |
|---|---|---|---|
| Regulation E (12 CFR 1005) | In force | Unauthorized electronic fund transfers; error resolution; disclosures; consent to recurring transfers | Civil liability and regulator penalties (verify) |
| Regulation Z (12 CFR 1026) | In force | Credit billing-error resolution; credit changes need disclosure | Per CFPB enforcement (verify) |
| UDAAP (CFPB Act Sections 1031 and 1036; FTC Act Section 5) | In force | Unfair, deceptive or abusive acts, including a bot that makes promises it cannot keep | Per CFPB and FTC orders (Section 1.4) |
| E-SIGN Act and UETA | In force | Electronic consent and signatures are valid, but authority and record-keeping still apply | Contract defenses (verify) |
| Agency law | Common law | The business is generally bound by actions its agent takes within authority. Limits and confirmation steps reduce disputed actions (verify by state) | Contract and tort exposure |
| Card-network and PCI rules (Section 3.1) | Contractual | Human approval for agent-initiated payment actions (verify current PCI guidance) | Acquirer and card-brand penalties |

Test: Q8 (money movement and account changes).

## 3.20 Retention versus deletion

Retention rules, deletion rights and legal holds can point in opposite directions. Decide each case under a written policy.

| Rule | Status | What it means | Possible penalties |
|---|---|---|---|
| GDPR Article 5(1)(e) and Article 17 | In force | Keep personal data only as long as needed; erase on request unless an exception applies | Up to EUR 20 million or 4% |
| CCPA and CPRA deletion rights | In force | Deletion on verified request, with exceptions for legal obligations, security and legal claims | Per Section 1.2 |
| NYC automated customer-service proposal | Pending | Three-year machine-readable logs of automated interactions, if adopted (Section 1.9.3) | Proposed penalties |
| Litigation holds and preservation orders | Common law and court orders | Preserve chat logs and recordings once litigation is reasonably anticipated; Federal Rule of Civil Procedure 37(e) on lost electronically stored information | Sanctions |
| PCI DSS v4.0.1, Requirement 10 | In force | Audit-log retention for at least 12 months, with 3 months immediately available (verify) | Acquirer and card-brand penalties |
| HIPAA documentation retention (45 CFR 164.316) | In force | Six years for required documentation (verify) | HHS civil penalties |

Conflict to decide in advance: a deletion request can collide with a legal hold, a three-year log rule or a PCI log rule. Document the decision and keep only what the exception requires. Test: Q3.

### US states

- **Massachusetts AG v. Earnest Operations** ($2.5M, July 2025). The mass.gov release did not load in this session, so the facts and terms rest on background knowledge.
- **Texas v. Allstate/Arity.** The filing date, court and current status are unconfirmed; the Texas AG page did not load.
- **August 2025 multistate AG letter on child safety.** The 44-AG count, the list of recipients and any later multistate actions need a primary source (NAAG or an AG release).
- **Kansas and the Oct 14, 2026 OpenAI suits.** Whether Kansas joined is unconfirmed, as is the full list of states.
- **Alabama.**
  - The HB 172 effective date conflicts across sources (2024 or 2026).
  - One source reports an Alabama Personal Data Protection Act (HB 351, signed Apr 16, 2026); it is unconfirmed.
- **Arizona.** Partly verified on November 22, 2026 (see the review table in Part 4):
  - the 2024 election-deepfake law is SB 1359 (Laws 2024, ch. 199), codified at A.R.S. 16-1024, per the official text; a secondary source's HB 2394 and 16-1023 attribution is not supported;
  - HB 2175 of 2025 (medical-director review of claim denials, no reliance solely on AI).
- **Arkansas "Right to Compute."** No such Arkansas act appears in the 2025 enacted list. Montana SB 212 is the confirmed one.
- **Idaho S 1297.** Confirm the customer-service exemption and the effective date against the enrolled text.
- **Nebraska LB 525.** Confirm the Conversational AI Safety Act duties, penalties and effective date.
- **Effective dates and enforcement provisions not confirmed:**
  - Colorado HB 26-1139;
  - Iowa HF 2635;
  - Louisiana HB 475;
  - Rhode Island HB 7349 and HB 7538;
  - Washington HB 1170;
  - California SB 867, SB 503 and SB 1050 (penalty provisions).
- **NAIC bulletin adoptions.** Recheck the adoption map (status Nov 3, 2026) for each state cited, especially ND Bulletin 2026-2 and Ohio.
- **New Hampshire RSA 507:8-k.** Does the "sole purpose ... open-ended generative communication" wording exclude customer-service bots?
- **AI voice under state telemarketing laws.** Whether an AI voice counts as "prerecorded" or "artificial" is untested in WI 100.52, MT 45-8-216, SC 37-21 ("first and last name"; 2-second rule) and SD 37-30A ("true name"). North Dakota's 51-28-02 expressly covers "synthesized voice."
- **Pending bill outcomes after the research date:**
  - NC HB 936 (last action June 2026);
  - Ohio SB 163 (lame duck);
  - Massachusetts S 2619 (conference, by Jan 5, 2027);
  - NY A 3411: vetoed November 20, 2026 (T1).
- **Vermont S.71.** Resolved: signed June 16, 2026 as Act 145; no 2026 veto (the reported June 8 veto concerned the 2024 bill H.121). Counts differ: MultiState (October 28, 2026) says 24 states after four 2026 enactments; Bloomberg Law lists Alabama, Louisiana, Oklahoma and Vermont as the 2026 signings and gives 23. The IAPP and Mayer Brown counts were not retrieved.
- **Connecticut PA 26-15.** Penalty amounts by section; one source shows $1,000 per violation for some duties.
- **Deepfake counts.** LawSignals counts election and political-ad AI laws enacted in 10 states in the 2025-26 sessions alone. A full count including 2023-24 laws (roughly two dozen or more states) was not rebuilt this round.
- **Private suits not verified this round** (state-law claims against chatbot makers): Garcia v. Character Technologies (M.D. Fla.), Raine v. OpenAI (San Francisco Superior Court) and similar cases.
- **State insurance regulator actions on AI claim denials.** None found. Confirm with state insurance department enforcement pages.
- **Federal preemption.** No state AI law was found to have been invalidated under EO 14365 (Dec 2025). Recheck the status of any DOJ AI Litigation Task Force suits.
- **Utah** was not re-reviewed (covered in the Utah subsection of Section 1.2).

# Part 5. Sources

**Source tiers:** T1 means the statute, Federal Register, official regulator or court page. T2 means a law-firm, news, tracker or vendor source. Trackers such as RecordingLaw, LawSignals, CASRAI and ailawsuittracker support only T2 claims. Rows cite their sources inline; the links below collect them by part.
